Z\;\operatorname{---}\;\mathscr{S}_{a}\xrightarrow{\phantom{x}\phi_{a}\phantom{x}}\overline{X_{a}}

where
Z
is the link of
X_{a}
in
X,

iii)

a hybrid atlas on
X
induces a hybrid atlas on

[X;\overline{X_{a}}]:=(X\setminus\overline{X_{a}})\sqcup\mathscr{S}_{a}

endowed with the poset stratification
[X;\overline{X_{a}}]\longrightarrow A^{\prime}
where

A^{\prime}=A\quad\text{ with }\quad A^{\prime}_{\mathrm{res}}=A_{\mathrm{res}}\cup\{a\},\quad A^{\prime}_{\mathrm{sing}}=A_{\mathrm{sing}}\setminus\{a\}

and where the strata are

[X;\overline{X_{a}}]_{b}=\begin{cases}\overline{X_{b}\setminus\overline{X_{a}}}&\text{ if }b<a\\
\mathscr{S}_{a}&\text{ if }b=a\\
X_{b}&\text{ otherwise }\end{cases}

iv)

if there is a stratum
X_{b}
such that
X_{b}\cap\overline{X_{a}}\neq\emptyset
(necessarily
b<a
) and

X_{b}\xrightarrow{\phantom{x}\phi_{b}\phantom{x}}B_{b}X,\quad[X;\overline{X_{a}}]_{b}\xrightarrow{\phantom{x}\widetilde{\phi}_{b}\phantom{x}}B_{b}X

are the corresponding fiber bundles, then

[X;\overline{X_{a}}]_{b}\cap\mathscr{S}_{a}=\phi_{a}^{-1}(\overline{X}_{a}\cap X_{b})

participates in the commutative diagram

\textstyle{[X;\overline{X_{a}}]_{b}\cap\mathscr{S}_{a}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\phi_{a}}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{\phi}_{b}}
\textstyle{\overline{X}_{a}\cap X_{b}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\phi_{b}}
\textstyle{B_{b}X}

Proof.

We proceed by induction on the depth of
A.
If the depth of
A
is zero then there is nothing to prove.

i) Let us denote the link of
X_{a}
in
X
by
Z,
so that each point in
X_{a}
has a stratified chart that identifies a neighborhood with
\mathbb{R}^{h}\times C(Z).
Our immediate goal is to show that points in the closure of
X_{a}
have compatible charts with image in
(\mathbb{R}^{+})^{h}\times C(Z).

Note that here to ease the notational burden we will use charts into
(\mathbb{R}^{+})^{h}\times C(Z)
with the understanding that they are homeomorphisms onto their image but while we assume that this image projects onto the second factor we do not assume that it is onto the first factor.
Thus when we use
(\mathbb{R}^{+})^{h}
we are allowing corners up to codimension
h
but not asserting that this codimension is realized.

If we recall that a smooth map defined on
(\mathbb{R}^{+})^{h}
means the restriction of a smooth map defined on some open set in
\mathbb{R}^{h},
then our notion
of smooth map between the images of charts of this form is as one would expect.

We know that strata in
A_{\mathrm{sing}}
whose closures have non-trivial intersection satisfy the frontier condition, so if
\zeta\in\overline{X}_{a}\setminus X_{a}
is contained in
X_{b}
then
b\in A_{\mathrm{res}}.
If

\varphi:\cal U\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\cal V\times W

is a res-chart of
X_{b}
centered at
\zeta,
where we are
using the notation from Definition
2.8
, then
\varphi(\cal U\cap\overline{X_{a}})
is of the form
\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\cal V\times W^{\prime}
for some stratum
W^{\prime}
of
W.
Now
W
is itself a hybrid space and since approaching
W^{\prime}
from
W\setminus W^{\prime}
corresponds via
\varphi
to approaching
\overline{X_{a}}
from
X\setminus\overline{X_{a}},

W^{\prime}
must be a stratum in the ‘
\mathrm{sing}
’ part of the partition of the strata of
W.
Thus points in
W^{\prime}
have sing-charts in
W
of the form

\varphi^{\prime}:\cal V^{\prime}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}^{h^{\prime}}\times C(L)
for some
h^{\prime}
and
L
(the link of
W^{\prime}
in
W
). Let
\cal U^{\prime}\subseteq X
be
\varphi^{-1}(\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\cal V\times\cal V^{\prime}).

By combining
\varphi
with an open embedding of
\cal V
into an appropriate
(\mathbb{R}^{+})^{h^{\prime\prime}}
and with
\varphi^{\prime}
we obtain

\psi:\cal U^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\phantom{x}\varphi\phantom{x}}\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\cal V\times\cal V^{\prime}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}^{+}\times(\mathbb{R}^{+})^{h^{\prime\prime}}\times\mathbb{R}^{h^{\prime}}\times C(L).

Since the restriction of
\psi
to
\cal U^{\prime}\cap X_{a}
is a coordinate chart (a sing-chart for
X_{a}
) we see that
1+h^{\prime}+h^{\prime\prime}=h
and
L=Z.

To simplify the notation we assume that the image of
\psi
lies in
\mathbb{R}^{+}\times(\mathbb{R}^{+})^{h^{\prime\prime}}\times(\mathbb{R}^{+})^{h^{\prime}}\times C(Z)=(\mathbb{R}^{+})^{h}\times C(Z)
(by composing with a stratified diffeomorphism if necessary) and we refer to

\psi:\cal U^{\prime}\longrightarrow(\mathbb{R}^{+})^{h}\times C(Z),

as an extended coordinate chart.

Note that

(2.3)

\psi(\cal U^{\prime}\cap\overline{X_{a}})\subseteq(\mathbb{R}^{+})^{h}\times\{*\}

(since
\varphi^{\prime}(W^{\prime})\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{h^{\prime}}\times\{*\}
) and that every point in
\overline{X}_{a}\setminus X_{a}
is in the domain of such an extended coordinate chart. These charts and the compatibility they inherit
from that of the charts of
X
give
\overline{X_{a}}
the structure of a smooth manifold with corners.

ii) Let
\mathscr{A}
be the union of a hybrid atlas for
X
and the extended
coordinate charts for points in
\overline{X}_{a}\setminus X_{a}
from (i),
and let
\mathscr{A}_{a}
be the subset of those charts centered at points in
\overline{X_{a}},

\mathscr{A}_{a}=\{(\cal U\xrightarrow{\phantom{x}\varphi\phantom{x}}(\mathbb{R}^{+})^{h}\times C(Z))\in\mathscr{A}:\varphi^{-1}((\mathbb{R}^{+})^{h}\times\{*\})\subseteq\overline{X_{a}}\}.

Let us consider
\mathbb{R}^{+}\longrightarrow[1]
as a smooth atlas stratified space with poset
\{0<1\}
and
\mathbb{R}^{+}_{0}=\{0\},
and let

\mathscr{P}_{a}
be the paths in
X
that start at
\overline{X_{a}}
and immediately move on to ‘higher’ strata,

\mathscr{P}_{a}=\{\chi:\mathbb{R}^{+}\longrightarrow X\text{ smooth stratified map}:\chi(t)\in\overline{X_{a}}\iff t=0\}.

By definition of smooth stratified maps, whenever
(\cal U\xrightarrow{\phantom{x}\varphi\phantom{x}}(\mathbb{R}^{+})^{h}\times C(Z))\in\mathscr{A}_{a}
and
\chi\in\mathscr{P}_{a},

\varphi\circ\chi
has a lift

\textstyle{(\mathbb{R}^{+})^{h}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}\times Z\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbb{R}^{+}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varphi\circ\chi}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{\varphi\circ\chi}}
\textstyle{(\mathbb{R}^{+})^{h}\times C(Z)}

Let us define an equivalence relation on
\mathscr{P}_{a}
by

\chi_{1}\sim\chi_{2}\iff\begin{cases}\chi_{1}(0)=\chi_{2}(0)&\text{ and }\\
$ $\\
\widetilde{\varphi\circ\chi_{1}}(0)=\widetilde{\varphi\circ\chi_{2}}(0)&\text{ for all charts in $\mathscr{A}_{a}$ including $\chi_{1}(0)$}\end{cases}

and denote the set of equivalence classes by
\mathscr{S}_{a},

\mathscr{S}_{a}=\mathscr{P}_{a}\diagup\sim.

To give the set
\mathscr{S}_{a}
some more structure, first note that there is an obvious map

\phi_{a}:\mathscr{S}_{a}\longrightarrow\overline{X_{a}},\quad[\chi]\mapsto\chi(0).

Next, given
(\cal U\xrightarrow{\phantom{x}\varphi\phantom{x}}(\mathbb{R}^{+})^{h}\times C(Z))\in\mathscr{A}_{a},
let us denote

\cal U_{a}=\cal U\cap\overline{X_{a}},\quad\varphi_{a}=\varphi|_{\cal U_{a}}:\cal U_{a}\longrightarrow(\mathbb{R}^{+})^{h}

and then

(2.4)

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 25.90219pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-25.90219pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\widetilde{\varphi}_{a}:\phi_{a}^{-1}(\cal U_{a})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 49.90219pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 49.90219pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{(\mathbb{R}^{+})^{h}\times Z,}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern-8.90623pt\raise-9.49501pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{[\chi]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 8.90625pt\raise-9.49501pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@stopper}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 64.09723pt\raise-9.49501pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 64.09723pt\raise-9.49501pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\widetilde{\varphi\circ\chi}(0)}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\text{ and }\varphi_{\mathscr{S}_{a}}=(\varphi_{a}^{-1}\times\operatorname{id})\circ\widetilde{\varphi}_{a}:\phi_{a}^{-1}(\cal U_{a})\longrightarrow\cal U_{a}\times Z.

If
(\cal U^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\phantom{x}\varphi^{\prime}\phantom{x}}(\mathbb{R}^{+})^{h}\times C(Z))\in\mathscr{A}_{a}
is another chart with
\cal U^{\prime}\cap\overline{X_{a}}=\cal U_{a}
then, on
\cal U\cap\cal U^{\prime},

\varphi^{\prime}=f\circ\varphi
for some stratified diffeomorphism whose lift

(2.5)

\textstyle{(\mathbb{R}^{+})^{h}\times\mathbb{R}\times Z\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\supseteq}
\textstyle{\cal V\times\mathbb{R}\times Z\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{f}}
\textstyle{\cal V^{\prime}\times\mathbb{R}\times Z\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\subseteq}
\textstyle{(\mathbb{R}^{+})^{h}\times\mathbb{R}\times Z}
\textstyle{(\mathbb{R}^{+})^{h}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}\times Z\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\supseteq}
\textstyle{\cal V\times\mathbb{R}^{+}\times Z\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{f}_{\geq 0}}
\textstyle{\cal V^{\prime}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}\times Z\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\subseteq}
\textstyle{(\mathbb{R}^{+})^{h}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}\times Z}
\textstyle{(\mathbb{R}^{+})^{h}\times C(Z)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\supseteq}
\textstyle{\cal V\times C(Z)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{\cal V^{\prime}\times C(Z)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\subseteq}
\textstyle{(\mathbb{R}^{+})^{h}\times C(Z)}

restricts to a stratified diffeomorphism
\cal V\times\{0\}\times Z\longrightarrow\cal V^{\prime}\times\{0\}\times Z

of the form
(y,0,z)\mapsto(\widetilde{f}^{\prime}(y),0,\widetilde{f}^{\prime\prime}(y,z)).

It follows that
\varphi_{\mathscr{S}_{a}}^{\prime}:\phi_{a}^{-1}(\cal U_{a})\longrightarrow\cal U_{a}\times Z
is given by

\phi_{a}^{-1}(\cal U_{a})\xrightarrow{\phantom{x}\varphi_{\mathscr{S}_{a}}\phantom{x}}\cal U_{a}\times Z\xrightarrow{\phantom{x}\widetilde{f}^{\prime\prime\prime}\phantom{x}}\cal U_{a}\times Z,\text{ where }\widetilde{f}^{\prime\prime\prime}(u,z)=(u,\widetilde{f}^{\prime\prime}(\varphi_{a}(u),z))

and hence differs from
\varphi_{\mathscr{S}_{a}}
by a stratified diffeomorphism.
Thus we may, without ambiguity, give
\mathscr{S}_{a}
the structure of a hybrid stratified space by requiring that all of the maps

\varphi_{\mathscr{S}_{a}}
are hybrid diffeomorphisms.
Finally, over each
\cal U_{a}
we have a commutative diagram

\textstyle{\phi_{a}^{-1}(\cal U_{a})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varphi_{\cal S_{a}}}
\scriptstyle{\phi_{a}}
\textstyle{\cal U_{a}\times Z\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\cal U_{a}}

with the arrow on the right given by the projection onto the first factor, so
\phi_{a}:\mathscr{S}_{a}\longrightarrow\overline{X_{a}}
is a stratified fiber bundle with fiber
Z.

iii)
Next we define the blow-up of
X
along
\overline{X_{a}}
to be

[X;\overline{X_{a}}]=(X\setminus\overline{X_{a}})\sqcup\mathscr{S}_{a}

together with a blow-down map

\beta:[X;\overline{X_{a}}]\longrightarrow X

given by collapsing the fibers of
\phi_{a}.
We define a poset stratification
[X;\overline{X_{a}}]\longrightarrow A
by

[X;\overline{X_{a}}]_{b}=\begin{cases}\text{ closure of }\beta^{-1}(X_{b}\setminus\overline{X_{a}})&\text{ if }b<a\\
\mathscr{S}_{a}&\text{ if }b=a\\
\beta^{-1}(X_{b})&\text{ otherwise }\end{cases}

(which is just a more careful version of the statement in the proposition). We will show that a hybrid atlas for
X
induces a hybrid atlas for
[X;\overline{X_{a}}]

with
a
moved from the ‘sing’ part of the partition of
A
to the ‘res’ part. That is, we will show that we can produce a hybrid atlas for
[X;\overline{X_{a}}]
for which points in a stratum indexed by
A_{\mathrm{res}}\cup\{a\}
have res-charts and point in a stratum indexed by
A_{\mathrm{sing}}\setminus\{a\}
have sing-charts.

First let us produce res-charts for the new res stratum
[X;\overline{X_{a}}]_{a}=\mathscr{S}_{a}.

The interesting charts we need to consider are the ones obtained from
\mathscr{A}
as follows.
If
(\cal U\xrightarrow{\phantom{x}\varphi\phantom{x}}(\mathbb{R}^{+})^{h}\times C(Z))\in\mathscr{A}_{a}
then, recalling the definition of
\widetilde{\varphi}_{a}
from (
2.4
), we let
\cal U_{a}=\cal U\cap\overline{X_{a}}
and

\widehat{\cal U}=(\cal U\setminus\cal U_{a})\sqcup\phi_{a}^{-1}(\cal U_{a}),\quad\widehat{\varphi}=\varphi|_{\cal U\setminus\cal U_{a}}\sqcup\widetilde{\varphi}_{a}:\widehat{\cal U}\longrightarrow(\mathbb{R}^{+})^{h}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}\times Z

where we are identifying
\mathrm{Image}(\widetilde{\varphi}_{a})\subseteq(\mathbb{R}^{+})^{h}\times Z
as a subset of
(\mathbb{R}^{+})^{h}\times\{0\}\times Z.

Let us consider the behavior of this construction when changing coordinate chart. Suppose
(\cal U^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\phantom{x}\varphi^{\prime}\phantom{x}}(\mathbb{R}^{+})^{h}\times C(Z))\in\mathscr{A}_{a}
is another chart with
\cal U\cap\cal U^{\prime}\neq\emptyset

then, on
\cal U\cap\cal U^{\prime},

\varphi^{\prime}=f\circ\varphi
for some stratified diffeomorphism participating in a diagram like (
2.5
)
and, since
f|_{\cal V\times(0,\infty)\times Z}=\widetilde{f}|_{\cal V\times(0,\infty)\times Z}
and
\widetilde{\varphi}^{\prime}=\widetilde{f}|_{\cal V\times\{0\}\times Z}\circ\widetilde{\varphi},
we have

\widehat{\varphi}^{\prime}=\widetilde{f}_{\geq 0}\circ\widehat{\varphi}.

Thus
\hat{\varphi}
and
\hat{\varphi}^{\prime}
are compatible res-charts for
[X;\overline{X_{a}}].

(Although we started our induction with the case of depth zero, we note that the proof thus far could be used to establish the case of depth one as the base of the induction.)

Now let us consider a stratum
[X;\overline{X_{a}}]_{b},

b\neq a.

For
b\in A_{\mathrm{sing}},
the pull-back of a sing-chart from
X
to
[X;\overline{X_{a}}]
is again a sing-chart. Indeed, a sing-chart centered at a point
\zeta\in X_{b}
is a stratified diffeomorphism of the form
\varphi:\cal U\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}^{\ell}\times C(W)
with
\cal U\subseteq X\setminus\overline{X_{a}}
and, since
\beta
restricts to the identity map between
[X;\overline{X_{a}}]\setminus[X;\overline{X_{a}}]_{a}
and
X\setminus\overline{X}_{a},

\varphi\circ\beta:\beta^{-1}(\cal U)\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}^{\ell}\times C(W)
is a sing-chart. Similarly compatibility between these sing-charts is clear because their overlap takes place in
X\setminus\overline{X_{a}}
and so is untouched by the blow-up of
\overline{X_{a}}.

It remains to consider a stratum
[X;\overline{X_{a}}]_{b},
with
b\in A_{\mathrm{res}}.
If
[X;\overline{X_{a}}]_{b},
does not intersect the new ‘front face’
[X;\overline{X_{a}}]_{a}=\mathscr{S}_{a}
then res-charts of
X_{b}
lift to res-charts of
[X;\overline{X_{a}}]_{b}
essentially unchanged, so let us assume that
[X;\overline{X_{a}}]_{b}\cap\mathscr{S}_{a}\neq\emptyset,
i.e., that
X_{b}\cap\overline{X_{a}}\neq\emptyset.
Let us denote the fiber bundle of
X_{b}
by

W\;\operatorname{---}\;X_{b}\xrightarrow{\phantom{x}\phi_{b}\phantom{x}}B_{b}X.

The discussion in (i) shows that the restriction of
\phi_{b}
to
X_{b}\cap\overline{X_{a}}
still fibers over
B_{b}X
and (as we did above) we denote the fiber by
W^{\prime},
thus

W^{\prime}\;\operatorname{---}\;X_{b}\cap\overline{X_{a}}\xrightarrow{\phantom{x}\phi_{b}|_{X_{b}\cap\overline{X_{a}}}\phantom{x}}B_{b}X.

Since
X_{a}
is a minimal sing-stratum in the stratification of
X,

W^{\prime}
is a minimal sing-stratum in the stratification of
W
and so our inductive hypothesis applies and we know that
[W;W^{\prime}]
is a hybrid space.
It follows that blowing-up
\overline{X_{a}}
in
X
has the effect of blowing-up
W^{\prime}
in each fiber of
\phi_{b}
and so produces the fiber bundle

[W;W^{\prime}]\;\operatorname{---}\;[X;\overline{X_{a}}]_{b}\xrightarrow{\phantom{x}\widetilde{\phi_{b}}:=\phi_{b}\circ\beta\phantom{x}}B_{b}X.

A trivialization of
\phi_{b}
lifts to a trivialization of
\widetilde{\phi_{b}}
and correspondingly a lift of a res-chart
\varphi:\cal U\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\cal V\times W
for
X_{b}
lifts to a res-chart
\widetilde{\varphi}:\widetilde{\cal U}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\cal V\times[W;W^{\prime}]
for
[X;\overline{X_{a}}]_{b}.

iv) Finally let us consider the relation between the fiber bundles
\phi_{a}
and
\widetilde{\phi}_{b}.

We have seen how blowing-up
\overline{X_{a}}
in
X
has the effect of blowing-up
W^{\prime}
in each fiber
W
of
\phi_{b}.

The ‘front face’ of
[X;\overline{X_{a}}],
i.e., the boundary hypersurface produced by the blow=up,
[X;\overline{X_{a}}]_{a},
thus corresponds to the front face of
[W;W^{\prime}]
in each fiber of
\widetilde{\phi}_{b}.
That is to say that
\widetilde{\phi}_{b}
restricts to a fiber bundle

\mathrm{ff}[W;W^{\prime}]\;\operatorname{---}\;[X;\overline{X_{a}}]_{b}\cap[X;\overline{X_{a}}]_{a}\xrightarrow{\phantom{x}\widetilde{\phi_{b}}\phantom{x}}B_{b}X.

On the other hand, since
\phi_{a}
is equal to the restriction of
\beta
to
[X;\overline{X_{a}}]_{a}
we have

\phi_{a}\big([X;\overline{X_{a}}]_{b}\cap[X;\overline{X_{a}}]_{a}\big)=X_{b}\cap\overline{X_{a}}.

Thus we have the commutative diagram

\textstyle{[X;\overline{X_{a}}]_{a}\cap[X;\overline{X_{a}}]_{b}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\phi_{a}}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{\phi}_{b}}
\textstyle{\overline{X_{a}}\cap X_{b}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\phi_{b}|_{\overline{X_{a}}\cap X_{b}}}
\textstyle{B_{b}X}

as required.
∎

If
X\longrightarrow A
is a smooth atlas stratified space and then repeated applications of this proposition produce
the resolution of
X,

\mathrm{res}(X),

a smooth manifold with corners with a boundary stratification parametrized by
A.

That is to say,

\mathrm{res}(X):=[\cdots[X;\overline{X_{a_{0}}}];\overline{X_{a_{1}}}];\cdots;\overline{X_{a_{n}}}],

where
a_{0},\ldots,a_{n}
is any non-decreasing enumeration of
A.
(It is easy to see that the result is independent of the order, up to a fibered corner
diffeomorphism.)
The natural map

\beta_{X}:\mathrm{res}(X)\longrightarrow X,

known as the blow-down map, is the composition of the blow-down maps of the individual blow-ups.

Theorem 2.10
.

Whenever
X\longrightarrow A
is a smooth atlas stratified space, its resolution
\mathrm{res}(X)
inherits from
X
the structure of a manifold with fibered corners with boundary stratification parametrized by
A.

The restriction of
\beta_{X}:\mathrm{res}(X)\longrightarrow X,
the blow-down map, to
\mathrm{res}(X)^{\circ}
is a diffeomorphism onto
X_{\mathrm{reg}}.

Moreover, for each
a\in A
we have

(2.6)

\partial_{a}\mathrm{res}(X)=\beta_{X}^{-1}(\overline{X_{a}}),

and, if
Z
is the link of
X_{a}
in
X,
then the corresponding fiber bundle satisfies

F_{a}\mathrm{res}(X)=\mathrm{res}(Z)\;\operatorname{---}\;\partial_{a}\mathrm{res}(X)\xrightarrow{\phantom{x}\phi_{a}\phantom{x}}B_{a}\mathrm{res}(X)=\mathrm{res}(\overline{X_{a}}),\quad\phi_{a}|_{(\partial_{a}\mathrm{res}(X))^{\circ}}=\beta_{X}|_{(\partial_{a}\mathrm{res}(X))^{\circ}}.

Whenever
M\longrightarrow A
is a manifold with fibered corners whose boundary stratification is parametrized by
A
there is a smooth atlas stratified space
X\longrightarrow A

such that
M=\mathrm{res}(X).

Next,
suppose
X^{\prime}\longrightarrow A^{\prime}
is another smooth atlas stratified space and
f:X\longrightarrow X^{\prime}
is a stratified map such that

(2.7)

f(X_{\mathrm{reg}})\subseteq X^{\prime}_{\mathrm{reg}}.

Then
f
is a smooth stratified map if and only if there exists a smooth fibered corners map
\widetilde{f}:\mathrm{res}(X)\longrightarrow\mathrm{res}(X^{\prime}),
called the ‘lift’ of
f,
participating in a commutative diagram

(2.8)

\textstyle{\mathrm{res}(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{f}}
\scriptstyle{\beta_{X}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{res}(X^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\beta_{X^{\prime}}}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{X^{\prime}}

Every smooth fibered corners map satisfying

(2.9)

\widetilde{f}(\mathrm{res}(X)^{\circ})\subseteq\mathrm{res}(X^{\prime})^{\circ}

is the lift of one and only one smooth stratified map.

It follows that resolution induces an equivalence of categories between smooth atlas stratified spaces and smooth stratified maps satisfying (
2.7
) and manifolds with fibered corners and smooth fibered corners maps satisfying (
2.9
).

Proof.

The statements about the structure of
\mathrm{res}(X)
follow immediately from the proposition.
So too does that fact that every manifold with fibered corners
M
is the resolution of a smooth atlas stratified space.
Indeed note that blowing-up a minimal singular stratum of a hybrid space is reversible. So starting with
M
we can choose a maximal boundary stratum
and blow it down to obtain a hybrid space with one singular stratum. Iterating until we have blown-down all of the res-strata produces a smooth atlas stratified space whose resolution is, manifestly, equal to
M.

Next let us consider the statements about lifting maps. First note that two maps
\widetilde{f}
and
f
participating in (
2.8
) mutually determine
each other since the blow-down map identifies the interior of the manifold with corners with the regular part of the stratified space and these are each
dense in their corresponding space. Thus if a map has a lift, it is unique.

Given a smooth stratified map
f:X\longrightarrow X^{\prime}
and a minimal stratum
X_{a}
of
X
there is a stratum
X^{\prime}_{a^{\prime}}
of
X^{\prime}
such that
f(X_{a})\subseteq X^{\prime}_{a^{\prime}}.

It is easy to see that
f
will lift to a smooth map
\widetilde{f}:[X;\overline{X_{a}}]\longrightarrow[X^{\prime};X^{\prime}_{a^{\prime}}].
(We assume that
X_{a}
and
X^{\prime}_{a^{\prime}}
are singular strata for the sake of concreteness.)
Indeed, denote the blow-down maps by
\beta:[X;\overline{X_{a}}]\longrightarrow X
and
\beta^{\prime}:[X^{\prime};X^{\prime}_{a^{\prime}}]\longrightarrow X^{\prime}
and suppose
\cal U\xrightarrow{\phantom{x}\varphi\phantom{x}}\mathbb{R}^{h}\times C(Z)
and
\cal U^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\phantom{x}\varphi^{\prime}\phantom{x}}\mathbb{R}^{h^{\prime}}\times C(Z^{\prime})
are coordinate charts of
points in
X_{a},

X^{\prime}_{a^{\prime}},
respectively, such that
f(\cal U)\subseteq\cal U^{\prime}.
Each of these charts induce a chart of the corresponding blown-up space,

\widetilde{\cal U}\xrightarrow{\phantom{x}\widetilde{\varphi}\phantom{x}}\mathbb{R}^{h}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}\times Z
and
\widetilde{\cal U^{\prime}}\xrightarrow{\phantom{x}\widetilde{\varphi}^{\prime}\phantom{x}}\mathbb{R}^{h}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}\times Z^{\prime},
and by definition of
smoothness there is a lift
\widetilde{f}_{\cal U}
of
f|_{\cal U}
fitting into a commutative diagram

\textstyle{\widetilde{\cal U}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\beta}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{\varphi}}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{f}_{\cal U}}
\textstyle{\widetilde{\cal U^{\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\beta^{\prime}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{R}^{h}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}\times Z\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbb{R}^{h}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}\times Z^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{\varphi^{\prime}}^{-1}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{R}^{h}\times C(Z)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbb{R}^{h}\times C(Z^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\varphi^{\prime}}^{-1}}
\textstyle{\cal U\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varphi}
\scriptstyle{f|_{\cal U}}
\textstyle{\cal U^{\prime}}

From the definition of smooth atlas we see that this lift of
f|_{\cal U}
is independent of the particular coordinate chart used in its definition and hence lifting
f
one coordinate chart at a time produces a global lift
\widetilde{f}.

If
X
and
X^{\prime}
are hybrid spaces then the analogous argument, with greater notational complexity, shows that
f
lifts to
\widetilde{f}:[X;\overline{X_{a}}]\longrightarrow[X^{\prime};X^{\prime}_{a^{\prime}}].

In this way, lifting one blow-up at a time, we see that smooth stratified maps satisfying (
2.7
) lift to maps of the resolutions.
(Note that if (
2.7
) does not hold then the analogous result is usually false. Indeed if
f(X_{\mathrm{reg}})\subseteq X^{\prime}_{a^{\prime}}
then lifting

f
would require a smooth
\widetilde{f}
satisfying

\textstyle{\partial_{a^{\prime}}\mathrm{res}(X^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\phi_{a^{\prime}}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{res}(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{f}}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{\mathrm{res}(\overline{X^{\prime}_{a^{\prime}}})}

and this is clearly topologically obstructed.)

On the other hand given a smooth fibered corners map
\widetilde{f}:\mathrm{res}(X)\longrightarrow\mathrm{res}(X^{\prime}),
the definition of smoothness requires that
\widetilde{f}

sends the fibers of a boundary fiber bundle of
X
to the fibers of the corresponding boundary fiber bundle of
X^{\prime}
and so it descends to a map between
the spaces obtained by blowing-down these boundary faces. Successively blowing-down all of the boundary fiber bundles we end up with a map between

X
and
X^{\prime}
such that (
2.8
) commutes.

Finally note that uniqueness implies that the lift of a composition is the composition of the lifts. Thus resolution and blow-down are mutually inverse functors
of categories between smooth atlas stratified spaces and smooth stratified maps satisfying (
2.7
) and manifolds with fibered corners and smooth fibered corners maps satisfying (
2.9
).
∎

The idea of resolving a stratified space to a manifold with corners goes back to Thom
[
Tho69
]
.
For Thom-Mather stratified spaces (which we will discuss next) resolution was worked out by Verona
[
Ver84
, §6.3.1]
.
That one obtains a manifold with fibered corners was pointed out by Melrose, see
[
ALMP12
, §2]
where this is discussed following
[
BHS91
]
.

Resolution of ‘conically smooth’ spaces is studied in
[
AFT17
, §7]
where it is referred to as ”Unzip”.
In
[
AFT17
, Theorem 7.3.8]
the authors establish that Unzip is a functor, however see Remark
2.3
.

Below, we will find it useful to identify the
C^{*}
-algebra of continuous functions on a smooth atlas stratified space
X
with a
C^{*}
-subalgebra
of the continuous functions on
\mathrm{res}(X).

Specifically, we define

(2.10)

\cal C_{\Phi}(\mathrm{res}(X))=\{f\in\cal C(\mathrm{res}(X)):\text{ for all }a\in S(X)\text{ we have }f|_{\partial_{a}X}\in\phi_{a}^{*}\cal C(B_{a}X)\}

and it is easy to see that pull-back along the blow-down map
\beta_{X}:\mathrm{res}(X)\longrightarrow X
establishes an isomorphism of
*
-algebras between

\cal C(X)
and
\cal C_{\Phi}(\mathrm{res}(X)).
We see that the smooth functions
X\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}
are precisely the functions in
\cal C(X)
that pull-back
along
\beta_{X}
to be in
\cal C_{\Phi}^{\infty}(\mathrm{res}(X)):=\cal C_{\Phi}(\mathrm{res}(X))\cap\cal C^{\infty}(\mathrm{res}(X)).

2.4.
Smooth atlas stratified spaces and Thom-Mather stratified spaces

This section will not be used in the rest of the paper.
In this subsection we will show that the class of spaces we defined in Definition
2.2
coincide with Thom-Mather stratified spaces.
We will first show that Thom-Mather stratified spaces are examples of smooth atlas stratified spaces. The converse is easy thanks to the resolution
process.

Definition 2.11
.

A
Thom-Mather stratified space
consists of a triple
(X,\cal S,\cal T)
where:

i)

X
is a Hausdorff, locally compact topological space with a countable basis for its topology.

ii)

\cal S
is a locally finite family of locally closed subsets of
X,
known as the strata of
X,
each of which is a smooth manifold.
The strata cover
X
and satisfy the frontier condition

L,M\in\cal S\text{ and }L\cap\overline{M}\neq\emptyset\implies L\subseteq\overline{M}.

iii)

\cal T
contains, for each
M\in\cal S,
a triple
(T_{M},\pi_{M},\rho_{M})
in which
T_{M}
is an open neighborhood of
M
in
X,
and

\pi_{M}:T_{M}\longrightarrow M,\quad\rho_{M}:T_{M}\longrightarrow[0,\infty)

are continuous functions such that
\pi_{M}|_{M}=\mathrm{id}_{M}
and
\rho_{M}^{-1}(0)=M.
The neighborhood
T_{M}
is known as a tubular neighborhood
of
M
in
X
and the triple
(T_{M},\pi_{M},\rho_{M})
as the control data of
M
in
X,

The control data are compatible in that, if
L
and
M
are distinct strata such that
L\subseteq\overline{M}
and
T_{L,M}=T_{L}\cap M,

\pi_{L,M}=\pi_{L}|_{T_{L,M}},

\rho_{L,M}=\rho_{L}|_{T_{L,M}},
then

(\pi_{L,M},\rho_{L,M}):T_{L,M}\longrightarrow L\times(0,\infty)

is a smooth submersion and, if
N
is a third distinct stratum such that
M\subseteq\overline{N},
then for any

\zeta\in T_{L,M}\cap T_{L,N}\cap\pi_{M,N}^{-1}(T_{L,M})
we have

\pi_{L,M}\pi_{M,N}(\zeta)=\pi_{L,N}(\zeta),\quad\rho_{L,M}\pi_{M,N}(\zeta)=\rho_{L,N}(\zeta).

Remark 2.12
.

As mentioned above, the main reason we work with smooth atlas stratified spaces is that they make it easy to work with fiber bundles of stratified spaces.
An example of the complications that arise when working with Thom-Mather stratified spaces is that, while the product of Thom-Mather stratified spaces is
again a Thom-Mather stratified space,
the product of the Thom-Mather data is not Thom-Mather data for the product
(pointed out in, e.g.,

[
Ver84
, §1.2.9]
). For a simple example, consider
Y=[0,\infty)_{x}
as a Thom-Mather stratified space with strata
\{0\}
and
(0,\infty),
and with
Thom-Mather data

\begin{gathered}\cal T_{\{0\}}=[0,1),\quad\pi_{\{0\}}(x)=0,\quad\rho_{\{0\}}(x)=x,\\
\cal T_{(0,\infty)}=(0,\infty),\quad\pi_{(0,\infty)}(x)=x,\quad\rho_{(0,\infty)}=0.\end{gathered}

Now on
X=Y^{2}
with the product stratification, the product Thom-Mather data would be

\displaystyle\cal T_{(0,0)}=[0,1)_{x}\times[0,1)_{x^{\prime}},\quad\pi_{(0,0)}(x,x^{\prime})

\displaystyle=(0,0),\quad\rho_{(0,0)}(x,x^{\prime})=x+x^{\prime},

\displaystyle\cal T_{\{0\}\times(0,\infty)}=[0,1)_{x}\times(0,\infty)_{x^{\prime}},\quad\pi_{\{0\}\times(0,\infty)}(x,x^{\prime})

\displaystyle=(0,x^{\prime}),\quad\rho_{\{0\}\times(0,\infty)}(x,x^{\prime})=x,

\displaystyle\cal T_{(0,\infty)\times\{0\}}=(0,\infty)_{x}\times[0,1)_{x^{\prime}},\quad\pi_{(0,\infty)\times\{0\}}(x,x^{\prime})

\displaystyle=(x,0),\quad\rho_{(0,\infty)\times\{0\}}(x,x^{\prime})=x^{\prime},

\displaystyle\cal T_{(0,\infty)^{2}}=(0,\infty)^{2},\quad\pi_{(0,\infty)}(x,x^{\prime})

\displaystyle=(x,x^{\prime}),\quad\rho_{(0,\infty)^{2}}=0

and the problem is that this data does not satisfy the compatibility condition

\rho_{(0,0)}\circ\pi_{\{0\}\times(0,\infty)}=\rho_{(0,0)}\text{ on }\cal T_{(0,0)}\cap\cal T_{\{0\}\times(0,\infty)},

as this would imply, e.g., that
\rho_{(0,0)}(0,x^{\prime})=\rho_{(0,0)}(x,x^{\prime})
for all
(x,x^{\prime})
in this set. This makes the definition of a fiber bundle of Thom-Mather stratified
spaces inconvenient.

Given a Thom-Mather stratified space
(X,\cal S,\cal T),
we can naturally consider
\cal S
as a poset with
L\leq M\iff L\subseteq\overline{M}
and

X
as poset stratified by the obvious map
X\longrightarrow\cal S.

A
controlled map
between Thom-Mather stratified spaces
(X,\cal S_{X},\cal T_{X})
and
(Y,\cal S_{Y},\cal T_{Y})
is a continuous stratified map

f
between
X\longrightarrow\cal S_{X}
and
Y\longrightarrow\cal S_{Y}
such that, whenever
f(X_{a})\subseteq Y_{b},
the restriction

f|_{X_{a}}:X_{a}\longrightarrow Y_{b}

is a smooth map and (possibly after shrinking the size of the tubular neighborhoods) we have

f(T_{X_{a}})\subseteq T_{Y_{b}},\quad f\circ\pi_{X_{a}}=\pi_{Y_{b}}\circ f,\quad\rho_{X_{a}}=\rho_{Y_{b}}\circ f.

A controlled isomorphism is a bijective controlled map whose inverse is also controlled, hence it is in particular a homeomorphism that restricts
to a diffeomorphism on each stratum.

An important property of controlled isomorphisms, identified by Brasselet-Hector-Saralegi
[
BHS91
]
is that they lift under ‘déplissage’.
In particular this means that for every controlled isomorphism

f:\mathbb{R}^{h_{1}}\times C(Z_{1})\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}^{h_{2}}\times C(Z_{2})

there a controlled isomorphism

\widetilde{f}:\mathbb{R}^{h_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}\times Z_{1}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}^{h_{2}}\times\mathbb{R}\times Z_{2},

that makes the diagram

\textstyle{\mathbb{R}^{h_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}\times Z_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{f}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{R}^{h_{2}}\times\mathbb{R}\times Z_{2}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{R}^{h_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}\times Z_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbb{R}^{h_{2}}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}\times Z_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbb{R}^{h_{1}}\times C(Z_{1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{\mathbb{R}^{h_{2}}\times C(Z_{2}),}

where the descending vertical arrows are the quotient maps of the cones, commutative.
(We have taken this property and, for not necessarily bijective maps, made it the definition of smooth stratified maps in the setting of smooth atlas stratified spaces above.)

Theorem 2.13
.

Thom-Mather stratified spaces admit smooth stratified atlases such that controlled isomorphisms are smooth stratified diffeomorphisms.

Smooth atlas stratified spaces admit control data giving them the structure of a Thom-Mather stratified space.

Proof.

Since the statement is obvious for Thom-Mather stratified spaces of depth zero, let us assume inductively that we have established the statement of the proposition for stratified spaces of depth less than some
\ell\in\mathbb{N}.

Let us recall some of the well-known consequences of Thom’s first isotopy lemma
[
Ver84
, Theorem 2.6]
when applied to the Thom-Mather
control data. This lemma says that every proper continuous map
F:X\longrightarrow M
between a Thom-Mather stratified space
X
and a smooth manifold
M,

whose restriction to each stratum of
X
is a smooth submersion, is a locally trivial fibration. Thus such a map participates in a fiber bundle
whose fiber is a Thom-Mather stratified space and with structure group given by the controlled isomorphisms of the fiber.

If
M
is a stratum of a Thom-Mather stratified space with control data
(T_{M},\pi_{M},\rho_{M})
then
(possibly after shrinking
T_{M}
and scaling
\rho_{M}
) we can apply the isotopy lemma to recognize

F=(\pi_{M},\rho_{M})|_{T_{M}\setminus M}:T_{M}\setminus M\longrightarrow M\times(0,1),

which the definition of Thom-Mather spaces assures us is a smooth submersion when restricted to each stratum of
T_{M}\setminus M,

as a locally trivial fibration. If we denote the restriction of
\pi_{M}
to
H=\rho_{M}^{-1}(\tfrac{1}{2})
by
\phi_{M}:H\longrightarrow M
then
we can identify the typical fiber of
F
with
H\times(0,1),
recognize that
\phi_{H}
is a locally trivial fiber bundle, and, if we denote the typical fiber
of
\phi_{H}
by
Z,
finally conclude that
\pi_{M}:T_{M}\longrightarrow M
is a locally trivial fiber bundle with fiber
C_{[0,1)}(Z).

In more detail, for every point
p\in M
there is a neighborhood
\cal V\subseteq M
and a stratified homeomorphism

\psi:\pi_{M}^{-1}(\cal V)\longrightarrow\cal V\times C_{[0,1)}(Z)
participating in the commutative diagram

\textstyle{\pi_{M}^{-1}(\cal V)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\psi}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{M}}
\textstyle{\cal V\times C_{[0,1)}(Z)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\cal V}

where the unlabeled arrow is the obvious map. If
\psi^{\prime}:\pi_{M}^{-1}(\cal V)\longrightarrow\cal V\times C_{[0,1)}(Z)
is another such homeomorphism then
the transition map

\psi^{\prime}\circ\psi^{-1}:\cal V\times C_{[0,1)}(Z)\longrightarrow\cal V\times C_{[0,1)}(Z)

is the identity on the first factor and is a controlled isomorphism on the second factor. As mentioned above, this controlled isomorphism lifts to
a controlled isomorphism on the ‘déplissage’ and so, if the depth of
Z
is less than
\ell,
a stratified diffeomorphism thanks to the inductive
hypothesis.

Thus if
(X,\cal S,\cal T)
is a Thom-Mather stratified space of depth at most
\ell
then we obtain stratified charts from the trivializing neighborhoods of
these fiber bundle structures on the tubular neighborhoods of the strata of
X,
together with the coordinate charts of the regular part of
X.
The analysis
above shows that this is a smooth stratified atlas. Finally this same analysis shows that a controlled isomorphism is a smooth stratified diffeomorphism.

Next if we start with a smooth atlas stratified space
X\longrightarrow A
we can obtain control data by using its resolution
\beta:\mathrm{res}(X)\longrightarrow X.

This goes back at least to
[
Ver84
, §5-6]
but a nice exposition can be found in
[
KR23
, Proposition A.4]
, to which we refer the reader for details.
∎

Remark 2.14
.

Note that the unzip result in
[
AFT17
, §7]
should imply a categorical equivalence between the category of conically smooth spaces and stratified diffeomorphisms and the category of manifolds with fibered corners and fibered diffeomorphisms; however if this were true then the conically smooth maps would coincide with our smooth maps between smooth atlas stratified spaces and Remark
2.3
shows that this is not the case. (In particular
[
AFT17
, Conjecture 1.5.3]
is true and well-known for Thom-Mather stratified spaces and hence for smooth atlas stratified spaces.)

In
[
NV23
]
, it is shown that Thom-Mather stratified spaces are conically smooth spaces.
The authors do not make use of the resolution studied in
[
AFT17
, §7]
to relate the two types of spaces, perhaps because they
were unaware of the parallel resolution result in
[
Ver84
]
for Thom-Mather spaces.

In summary, we now know that

(1)

Thom-Mather stratified spaces,

(2)

Whitney embedded stratified spaces,

(3)

manifolds with fibered corners,

(4)

smooth atlas stratified spaces,

all describe the same singular spaces, which we refer to as smoothly stratified spaces.

2.5.
Witt spaces

In this short subsection we recall the main objects we will be studying throughout the rest of the paper.

Definition 2.15

(Witt spaces
[
Sie83
, Definition 2.1]
)
.

A smoothly stratified space
X
is a
Witt space
if whenever
Z
is the link in
X
of a singular stratum we either have that
\ell=\dim Z
is odd or that
\mathrm{IH}_{\ell/2}^{\overline{m}}(Z;\mathbb{Q})=0,
where
\mathrm{IH}^{\overline{m}}_{*}(Z;\mathbb{Q})
denotes the lower middle perversity intersection homology groups of
Z
with rational coefficients
[
GM80
, §5.1]
.

Siegel introduced Witt spaces, though he worked with PL stratified spaces. He introduced a bordism theory of oriented PL Witt
pseudomanifolds
7
7

7

Recall from §
2.2
that a pseudomanifold is a stratified space whose regular part is dense and has a fixed dimension and whose other strata have dimension at least two less than the regular part.
,
\Omega^{\operatorname{Witt}}_{*}(-),
which is now well known to be a homology theory. The name ‘Witt spaces’ comes from the fact that the bordism groups of a point can be computed to essentially equal the Witt groups of
\mathbb{Q};
specifically,
[
Sie83
, pg. 1068]

\Omega^{\operatorname{Witt}}_{q}(\mathrm{pt})=\begin{cases}\mathbb{Z}&\text{ if }q=0\\
0&\text{ if }q\not\equiv 0\mod 4\\
\mathrm{Witt}(\mathbb{Q})&\text{ if }q>0,\;q\equiv 0\mod 4\end{cases}

One can also consider the bordism theory based on cycles given by continuous maps on smoothly stratified oriented Witt pseudomanifolds,
\Omega^{{\operatorname{Witt}},\infty}_{*}(-).

We show in Proposition
4.5
that these are naturally equivalent.)

Natural examples of oriented Witt pseudomanifolds include
pure-dimensional complex algebraic varieties, or
indeed any stratified space all of whose strata are even-codimensional.

3.
The analytic orientation class of a Witt pseudomanifold

3.1.
K-homology orientations

Let
E
be a ring spectrum.
We review
E
-homological orientation classes of manifolds, see e.g.
Rudyak
[
Rud98
]
.
First, spheres
S^{n}
are
E
-oriented as follows.
Let
1\in\pi_{0}(E)
be the unit of the homotopy ring
\pi_{*}(E)
.
The homology theory
E_{*}
determined by
E
has a
suspension isomorphism

\sigma_{*}:\pi_{0}(E)=\widetilde{E}_{0}(S^{0})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\longrightarrow}}\widetilde{E}_{n}(S^{n})=E_{n}(S^{n},*).

One defines the orientation of
S^{n}
to be

[S^{n}]_{E}:=\sigma_{*}(1).

Now let
M
be an
n
-dimensional topological manifold, say without boundary.
For every point
p\in M
and every disc neighborhood

U\subset M
of
p
, let

\operatorname{coll}^{p,U}:M\to S^{n}
be the map which collapses the complement
of
U
. If
U^{\prime}
is another disc neighborhood of
p
, then

\operatorname{coll}^{p,U^{\prime}}
and
\operatorname{coll}^{p,U}
are homotopic.
Thus we obtain a well-defined homotopy class

\operatorname{coll}^{p}:M\to S^{n}.

Definition 3.1
.

An element
[M]_{E}\in E_{n}(M)
is called an
E
-orientation of
M
,
if

(3.1)

\operatorname{coll}^{p}_{*}[M]_{E}=\pm[S^{n}]_{E}

for every
p\in M
.

Suppose that
M
is connected.
Then the homotopy classes
\operatorname{coll}^{p}

and
\operatorname{coll}^{p^{\prime}}
coincide for any two points
p,p^{\prime}\in M

by choosing a path from
p
to
p^{\prime}
.
So it suffices for connected manifolds to verify
(
3.1
) at a single point
p
.
If
M
is
E
-oriented, then the Poincaré duality map

-\cap[M]_{E}:E^{k}(M)\longrightarrow E_{n-k}(M)

is an isomorphism.

Proposition 3.2
.

Let
\phi:E\to F
be a ring morphism of ring spectra and

M
an
n
-dimensional manifold. If
[M]_{E}\in E_{n}(M)

is an
E
-orientation of
M
, then its image
\phi_{*}[M]_{E}\in F_{n}(M)

is an
F
-orientation of
M
.

Proof.

The morphism
\phi
induces a natural transformation
\phi_{*}:E_{*}(-)\to F_{*}(-)

of homology theories. This transformation commutes with the induced homomorphisms

\operatorname{coll}^{p}_{*}
, and with the suspension isomorphisms.
Furthermore, it maps the unit
1\in\widetilde{E}_{0}(S^{0})
to the unit

1\in\widetilde{F}_{0}(S^{0})
. Therefore, for every
p\in M,

\operatorname{coll}^{p}_{*}\phi_{*}[M]_{E}=\phi_{*}\operatorname{coll}^{p}_{*}[M]_{E}=\pm\phi_{*}[S^{n}]_{E}=\pm\phi_{*}\sigma_{E,*}(1)=\pm\sigma_{F,*}\phi_{*}(1)=\pm\sigma_{F,*}(1)=\pm[S^{n}]_{F}.

∎

Since complexification and the stable second Adams operation are multiplicative,
Proposition
3.2
together with
Theorem
7.2
implies that the
(normalized) class
{\operatorname{sign}}_{K}(M)
of the signature operator
is a
{\operatorname{K}}[\mbox{$\frac{1}{2}$}]
-orientation for a closed smooth

H\mathbb{Z}
-oriented manifold
M
.
(It is not an orientation at
2
.)

3.2.
Wedge metrics and Dirac-type operators

We recall the definition of wedge Dirac-type operators from
[
AGR23
]
.
The example most important to us will be the signature operator of a wedge metric acting on wedge differential forms.

Let
M
be a manifold with fibered corners and let
M\longrightarrow S(M)
denote the corresponding boundary stratification.
Wedge one forms are defined by

\cal V_{w}^{*}=\{\theta\in\cal C^{\infty}(M;T^{*}M):\text{ for each $a\in S(M)$ and $y\in B_{a}M,$}\quad\theta|_{\phi_{a}^{-1}(y)}=0\}

and by the Serre-Swan theorem or by direct construction as in
[
Mel93
, §8.1-8.2]
there is a vector bundle

{}^{w}T^{*}M\longrightarrow M

endowed with a bundle map
i:{}^{w}T^{*}M\longrightarrow T^{*}M
with the property that

i_{*}\cal C^{\infty}(M;{}^{w}T^{*}M)=\cal V_{w}^{*}\subseteq\cal C^{\infty}(M;T^{*}M).

In particular,
i
is a bundle isomorphism over
M^{\circ}
and hence

{}^{w}T^{*}M|_{M^{\circ}}\text{ is canonically isomorphic to }T^{*}(M^{\circ}).

Since
M
is homotopic to
M^{\circ},
it follows that
{}^{w}T^{*}M
is isomorphic to
T^{*}M
over
M

(although not naturally and
i
is not an isomorphism over the boundary of
M
).
In local coordinates near the total space of
F_{a}M\;\operatorname{---}\;\partial_{a}M\xrightarrow{\phantom{x}\phi_{a}\phantom{x}}B_{a}M,

(x,y_{1},\ldots,y_{h},z_{1},\ldots,z_{v}),
in which
x
is a boundary defining function for
\partial_{a}M,

y_{j}
are coordinates pulled-back from
B_{a}M,
and
z_{k}
are coordinates on
F_{a}M,

a differential form

\theta=a(x,y,z)dx+b^{j}(x,y,z)dy_{j}+c^{k}(x,y,z)dz_{k}

is in
\cal V_{w}^{*}
if and only if the coefficients
c^{k}
vanish when
x=0
and so the differentials
dx,

dy_{1},\ldots,dy_{h},

x\;dz_{1},\ldots,x\;dz_{v}
make up a local
frame for
\cal V_{w}^{*}.

Abusing notation we think of elements of
\cal V_{W}^{*}
as sections of
{}^{w}T^{*}M.
Importantly though, a form like
x\;dz_{1}
does not vanish at
\{x=0\}
when thought
of as a section of
{}^{w}T^{*}M
(e.g., because
dz_{1}
is not a section of
{}^{w}T^{*}M
so
x\;dz_{1}
is not
x
times another section).

The wedge tangent bundle
{}^{w}TM
is the dual bundle to the wedge cotangent bundle. A general wedge metric can be defined as a bundle metric on
{}^{w}TM

but it is convenient to require more structure. Namely, a product-type wedge metric is one that near each
\partial_{a}M
has the form

dx^{2}+x^{2}g_{\partial_{a}M/B_{a}M}+\phi_{a}^{*}g_{B_{a}M}

where
g_{\partial_{a}M/B_{a}M}+\phi_{a}^{*}g_{B_{a}M}
is a submersion metric on
\partial_{a}M.

A totally geodesic wedge metric is one that coincides with a product-type wedge metric at each
\partial_{a}M
up to
\cal O(x^{2}).
(See
[
AGR23
, §1.2]

for more details, and also
[
KR23
, §6]
.)

For totally geodesic wedge metrics (and more generally) one can use the Koszul formula to see that the Levi-Civita connection defines a connection
on the wedge tangent bundle,

\nabla^{M}:\cal C^{\infty}(M;{}^{w}TM)\longrightarrow\cal C^{\infty}(M;T^{*}M\otimes{}^{w}TM),

and hence also on the wedge cotangent bundle.

Definition 3.3
.

Let
M
be a manifold with fibered corners and a wedge Riemannian metric
g_{M}.

A
wedge Clifford module
on
M
consists of a complex vector bundle
E\longrightarrow M
endowed with a Hermitian metric
g_{E},
a metric connection
\nabla^{E},

and a bundle homomorphism (referred to as Clifford multiplication)

\mathpzc{cl}:\mathbb{C}\otimes\mathrm{Cl}({}^{w}T^{*}M,g_{M})\longrightarrow\mathrm{End}(E)

satisfying that, for any
\theta\in\cal C^{\infty}(M;{}^{w}T^{*}M)
and
V\in\cal C^{\infty}(M;TM),

g_{E}(\mathpzc{cl}(\theta)\cdot,\cdot)=-g_{E}(\cdot,\mathpzc{cl}(\theta)\cdot)\quad\text{ and }\quad\nabla^{E}_{V}\mathpzc{cl}(\theta)=\mathpzc{cl}(\theta)\nabla^{E}_{V}+\mathpzc{cl}(\nabla^{M}_{V}\theta).

The corresponding Dirac-type operator is the differential operator given by the composition

\eth^{E}:\cal C^{\infty}_{c}(M^{\circ};E)\xrightarrow{\phantom{x}\nabla^{E}\phantom{x}}\cal C^{\infty}_{c}(M^{\circ};T^{*}M\otimes E)\xrightarrow{\phantom{x}\mathpzc{cl}\phantom{x}}\cal C^{\infty}_{c}(M^{\circ};E),

where we have used that
T^{*}M
and
{}^{w}T^{*}M
are canonically isomorphic over the interior of
M.

A
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-graded wedge Clifford module is a wedge Clifford module in which
E
has a splitting
E=E^{+}\oplus E^{-}

which is orthogonal with respect to
g_{E},
parallel with respect to
\nabla^{E},
and odd with respect to
\mathpzc{cl}.

Using the metrics on
M
and on
E
we can define
L^{2}(M;E)
and consider
\eth^{E}
as a symmetric
unbounded operator with initial domain
\cal C^{\infty}_{c}(M^{\circ};E).
Elliptic regularity in this setting establishes that any element
of
L^{2}(M;E)
that is mapped into
L^{2}(M;E)
by
\eth^{E}
(acting distributionally) must have Sobolev regularity but measured
using “edge vector fields”. An edge vector field on
M
is one that is tangent to the fibers of all of the boundary fiber bundles of the
iterated fibration structure on
M
and we denote the corresponding Sobolev space by
H^{1}_{e}(X;E).

Thus the elliptic regularity statement is that

\cal D_{\max}(\eth^{E}):=\{u\in L^{2}(M;E):\eth^{E}u\in L^{2}(M;E)\}\subseteq H^{1}_{e}(X;E).

For our purposes, the initial domain
\cal C^{\infty}_{c}(M^{\circ};E)
of
\eth^{E}
is too small and the maximal domain,
\cal D_{\max}(\eth^{E}),
is too large
so we use an intermediate domain known as the ‘vertical APS domain’ (so called because of its close relation to the domain introduced by Atiyah-Patodi-Singer in
[
APS75
]
).
This domain is defined as

\cal D_{VAPS}(\eth^{E}):=\rho^{1/2}H^{1}_{e}(X;E)\cap\cal D_{\max}(\eth^{E})

where
\rho
is a ‘total boundary defining function’, i.e., a smooth non-negative function that vanishes simply at the boundary of
M.

In
[
AGR23
]
this was shown to be a natural choice of domain for
\eth^{E}
and to have several nice properties
as long as an ‘analytic Witt assumption’ is satisfied.

To state this assumption, note that for any
\alpha\in S(M),
a wedge Dirac-type operator
\eth^{E}
takes the form

\frac{1}{x}(a(x,y,z)x\partial_{x}+b^{i}(x,y,z)x\partial_{y_{j}}+c^{k}(x,y,z)\partial_{z_{k}}+f(x,y,z))

near
\partial_{\alpha}M=\{x=0\}.
We refer to

\eth^{E}_{\alpha}=x\eth^{E}|_{\partial_{\alpha}M}=c^{k}(0,y,z)\partial_{z_{k}}+f(0,y,z)

as the
boundary family
of
\eth^{E}
at
\partial_{\alpha}M.
It is a family of operators on the fibers of
\phi_{\alpha}:\partial_{\alpha}M\longrightarrow B_{\alpha}M.

In fact it is practically equal to a family of Dirac-type operators with respect to an induced wedge Clifford module, see
[
AGR23
, Lemma 2.2]
.
We say that
\eth^{E}
satisfies the
analytic Witt condition
if every boundary family, endowed with its vertical APS domain, is invertible.

In
[
AGR23
, Theorem 1]
it is shown that if
M
is compact and
\eth^{E}
satisfies the analytic Witt condition then

(\eth^{E},\cal D_{VAPS}(\eth^{E}))
is closed
8
8

8

In the statement of
[
AGR23
, Theorem 1]
the vertical APS domain is defined as the graph closure of the domain defined above but the proof of
[
AGR23
, Theorem 4.3]
shows that the domain is already closed in the graph norm.
, Fredholm, and self-adjoint with compact resolvent. In case
M
is non-compact, but the links of the associated
stratified space are compact, the method described in
[
ALMP18
, §3]
and implemented in, e.g., the proof of

[
ALMP18
, Theorem 5.2]
shows that
(\eth^{E},\cal D_{VAPS}(\eth^{E}))
is closed, locally Fredholm, and self-adjoint with locally compact
resolvent
9
9

9

By locally compact we mean an operator whose product with any smooth function of compact support is a compact operator and by locally Fredholm we mean an operator that is invertible up to a locally compact operator.
.

The most important special case of this construction for us is the signature operator.
First if we replace the ordinary differential forms on
M
with the wedge differential forms, defined to be the sections of the exterior powers of the wedge cotangent bundle,

\cal C^{\infty}_{c}(M;\Lambda^{*}({}^{w}T^{*}M)),

then the de Rham operator
d+\delta
is a wedge Dirac-type operator. The analytic Witt assumption is satisfied by
d+\delta
if and only if
M
is (the resolution of) a Witt pseudomanifold in the sense of Siegel
[
Sie83
]
(this is why it is called the analytic Witt assumption); see e.g.,
[
ALMP18
, Corollary 4.2]
.

To define the signature operator we first replace wedge cotangent bundle with its complexification
{}^{w}T^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}M={}^{w}T^{*}M\otimes\mathbb{C}
and then
let
\star
be the involution obtained from the Hodge star
*
by multiplying it with an appropriate (form degree dependent) power of
i
so that
\star^{2}=1.

If
\dim M
is even then the signature operator
D_{M}^{\mathrm{sign}}=\eth_{\mathrm{sign}}
is the operator induced by the de Rham operator together with the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-grading induced by
\star.

If
\dim M
is odd then the de Rham operator commutes with
\star
and the signature operator (known as the ‘odd signature operator’) is its restriction to the
+1
eigenspace of
\star.
(In either case, the analytic Witt assumption for the signature operator holds precisely when the analytic Witt assumption for the de Rham operator does, i.e., when
M
is a Witt pseudomanifold.)

Summarizing, we have the following fundamental result:

Theorem 3.4
.

Let
X
be a smoothly stratified Witt pseudomanifold
and let
M=\mathrm{res}(X)
be its resolution, a manifold with fibered corners. We endow the regular part of

X
and thus the interior of
M
with a wedge metric
g
. Then
the signature operator
D_{M}^{\mathrm{sign}}
associated to
g

satisfies the analytic Witt condition and therefore admits
a closed self-adjoint extension
\cal D_{VAPS}(\eth_{\mathrm{sign}}))\subset L^{2}(M,\Lambda^{*}({}^{w}T^{*}M))

which is Fredholm and has compact resolvent.

For some wedge metrics on a Witt space, called adapted iterated edge metrics in
[
ALMP12
, §5.4]
, the de Rham operator is essentially self-adjoint as an unbounded operator on
L^{2}
differential forms. Any wedge metric can be deformed to one of this form and this deformation produces a homotopy of the de Rham (or signature) operators within Fredholm operators (see
[
AGR23
, Remark 4.9]
). Thus for some purposes, e.g., defining a K-homology class, one may if convenient assume that the operator is essentially self-adjoint.

3.3.
KK-theory and the analytic signature orientation class of a Witt space

We find it convenient to make use of the unbounded description of KK-theory set out by van den Dungen and Mesland in
[
vdDM20
]
.
For the reader’s convenience we start by reviewing some of the relevant notions.

Let
A
and
B
be countably generated,
\sigma
-unital,
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-graded
C^{*}
-algebras. A
B
-Hilbert module
\cal E
is a right
B
-module equipped with a
B
-valued inner product

\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\cal E}
which is complete with respect to the norm
\lVert v\rVert_{\cal E}=\lVert\langle v,v\rangle_{\cal E}\rVert_{B}^{1/2}.

A
B
-Hilbert module is an
(A,B)
-bimodule if
A
acts on it via adjointable bounded operators.

Let
D:\mathrm{dom}(D)\subseteq\cal E\longrightarrow\cal E
be a closed, densely defined, self-adjoint and regular
10
10

10

To say that
D
is regular is to say that
D
and
D^{*}
are densely defined and that
\mathrm{Id}+D^{*}D
has dense range.
operator and define

\mathrm{Lip}(D)=\{T\text{ adjointable, bounded operator on }E:\\
T(\mathrm{dom}(D))\subseteq\mathrm{dom}(D)\text{ and }[D,T]\text{ is adjointable and bounded}\},

\mathrm{Lip}_{\cal K}(D)=\{T\in\mathrm{Lip}(D):\\
T(\mathrm{Id}+D^{2})^{-1/2},T^{*}(\mathrm{Id}+D^{2})^{-1/2}\in\cal K_{B}\}.

An
unbounded
A
-
B
-cycle
11
11

11

An unbounded Kasparov module for
(A,B),
in the sense of Baaj-Julg
[
BJ83
]
is an
(A,B)
-bimodule
\cal E
together with a self-adjoint regular operator
D
on
E,
homogeneous of degree one, such that
(1+D^{2})^{-1}a
extends to an element of
\cal K_{B}
for all
a\in A
and such that there is a dense
*
-subalgebra
\cal A\subseteq A
contained in
\mathrm{Lip}(D).

A significant complication of using unbounded Kasparov modules is that in order to define the direct sum of two modules we would require that the subalgebra
\cal A
is the same for the two modules. This is avoided with unbounded
A
-
B
-cycles where instead it is required that
A\subseteq\overline{\mathrm{Lip}(D)}.
is a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-graded Hilbert bi-module
{}_{A}\cal E_{B}
together with an odd regular self-adjoint operator
D
on
E
such
that the elements of
A
act as operators in the closure of
\mathrm{Lip}_{\cal K}(D).
Direct sum makes the set of unbounded
A
-
B
-cycles into a semi-group.
The equivalence classes of unbounded
A
-
B
-cycles with respect to operator homotopy form a group with respect to direct sum denoted

\overline{\mathrm{UKK}}(A,B).

Van den Dungen and Mesland show that if
A
is separable the bounded transform induces an isomorphism

\overline{\mathrm{UKK}}(A,B)\cong\mathrm{KK}(A,B).

The odd KK-groups
\overline{\mathrm{UKK}}^{1}(A,B)\cong\mathrm{KK}^{1}(A,B)
are defined by the same procedure as above but working with ungraded Hilbert bi-modules. We will denote the even groups by
\overline{\mathrm{UKK}}(A,B)\cong\mathrm{KK}(A,B)
or
\overline{\mathrm{UKK}}^{0}(A,B)\cong\mathrm{KK}^{0}(A,B)
interchangeably. A superindex in this setting is to be understood modulo two.

Roughly speaking if
X
and
Y
are stratified spaces (and more generally) then

\begin{gathered}\mathrm{K}^{0}_{c}(X):=\mathrm{KK}(\mathbb{C},C_{0}(X))=\text{ ``Fredholm operators parametrized by $X$"},\\
\mathrm{K}_{0}^{{\operatorname{an}}}(X):=\mathrm{KK}(C_{0}(X),\mathbb{C})=\text{ ``Fredholm operators on $X$"},\\
\mathrm{KK}(C_{0}(X),C_{0}(Y))=\text{ ``Fredholm operators on $X$ parametrized by $Y$"}.\end{gathered}

In particular a wedge Dirac operator on
X
satisfying the analytic Witt condition will define a class in
\mathrm{K}_{0}^{{\operatorname{an}}}(X)
and,
as we will show in §
5
, a family of wedge Dirac-type operators on the fibers of
X\longrightarrow Y
satisfying the analytic Witt condition will define
a class in
\mathrm{KK}(C_{0}(X),C_{0}(Y)).

Let us describe the case of a single operator in detail. Suppose
X
is a smoothly stratified space (not necessarily compact) with resolution

M=\mathrm{res}(X),
that
(g_{M},E\longrightarrow M,g_{E},\nabla^{E},\mathpzc{cl})
is a wedge Clifford module on
M
and that the associated Dirac-type operator

\eth^{E}_{M}
satisfies the analytic Witt condition. Let us set

A=C_{\Phi,0}(M):=C_{\Phi}(M)\cap C_{0}(M),\quad B=\mathbb{C},\quad\cal E=L^{2}(M;E)

and endow
\cal E
with a left
A
-action and right
B
-action, both given by multiplication, and note that the
L^{2}
-inner product on
\cal E
is a

B
-valued inner product so that
\cal E
is a Hilbert
A
-
B
-bimodule,
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-graded if
E
is.
The operator
\eth^{E}_{M,VAPS}
is closed, densely defined, self-adjoint and regular and

C_{\Phi,c}^{\infty}(M):=C_{\Phi}^{\infty}(M)\cap C_{c}^{\infty}(M)\subseteq\mathrm{Lip}_{\cal K}(\eth^{E}_{M,VAPS})
so the elements of
A
act as operators in the closure of

\mathrm{Lip}_{\cal K}(\eth^{E}_{M,VAPS}).
Thus we obtain a class

[\eth^{E}_{M,VAPS}]\in\overline{\mathrm{UKK}}^{\dim X}(C_{\Phi,0}(M),\mathbb{C})\cong\mathrm{KK}^{\dim X}(C_{\Phi,0}(M),\mathbb{C})=\mathrm{KK}^{\dim X}(C_{0}(X),\mathbb{C})=\mathrm{K}_{\dim X}^{{\operatorname{an}}}(X).

The case of most interest to us is the
K
-class of the signature operator of a Witt pseudomanifold
X,

[D_{M}^{\mathrm{sign}}]\in\mathrm{K}_{\dim X}^{{\operatorname{an}}}(X).

For compatibility with the Sullivan orientation, as we will show below in §
7
, it is natural to define

\mathrm{sign}_{K}(X)=2^{-\lfloor\dim X/2\rfloor}[D_{M}^{\mathrm{sign}}]\in\mathrm{K}_{\dim X}^{{\operatorname{an}}}(X)[\tfrac{1}{2}].

We refer to this as the
analytic signature orientation of a Witt space
.

Remark 3.5
.

The class of the signature operator in
\mathrm{K}_{\dim X}^{{\operatorname{an}}}(X)
coincides with that defined in
[
ALMP12
, §6.2]
(and that defined in
[
MW97
]
). See also the definition of the K-homology class of the signature operator on Cheeger spaces (generalizing Witt spaces) in
[
ALMP17
, §5.1]
.

4.
Invariance properties of the analytic signature orientation

In this section we consider the behavior of the analytic signature orientation under changes of the underlying structure.

4.1.
Stratified diffeomorphism invariance

Proposition 4.1
.

The analytic signature orientation of a smoothly stratified oriented Witt space is independent of the choice of wedge metric used in its definition.

If
\phi:X\to Y
is a stratified diffeomorphism between
smoothly stratified oriented Witt spaces, then

\phi_{*}\mathrm{sign}_{K}(X)=\mathrm{sign}_{K}(Y)\in{\operatorname{K}}_{*}^{{\operatorname{an}}}(Y)=\mathrm{KK}^{*}(C_{0}(Y),\mathbb{C}).

Proof.

If
g
and
g^{\prime}
are wedge metrics on
X
then they are quasi-isometric and hence the space of
L^{2}
sections of wedge differential forms is the same
for both metrics (though of course the
L^{2}
-norm of an individual element is not). The path of wedge metrics
t\mapsto tg+(1-t)g^{\prime}
lifts to an operator homotopy between the corresponding signature operators, endowed with their VAPS domain. Since operator homotopies between Kasparov bimodules do not change the KK-class, we see that the analytic signature orientation is independent of the metric.

Let
g_{Y}
be a wedge metric on
Y
and consider
\phi^{*}g
on
X
. It can be proved that this is also a wedge metric.
This can certainly be proved directly as in
[
BHS92
, Lemme 5.2]
. However, it also follows from
the following general principle: a wedge metric
g_{Y}
extends to define a bundle metric on the wedge cotangent bundle over the resolved
manifold of
Y
,
\widetilde{Y}
; a stratified diffeomorphism
\phi
lifts to a diffeomorphism
\tilde{\phi}
of the resolutions
and thus induces a well-defined bundle map, covering
\tilde{\phi}
, between the respective wedge-cotangent bundles. The pull-back of the
metric
g_{Y}
is then a metric on the wedge-cotangent bundle of the resolved manifold
\widetilde{X}
; such a metric
defines on the interior of
\widetilde{X}
, which is the regular part of
X
, a wedge metric.

Consider any class
[H,\alpha:C(X)\to\mathcal{B}(H),F]\in\mathrm{K}_{*}^{{\operatorname{an}}}(X)
and recall
that if
\psi:X\to Y
is a continuous map, then

\psi_{*}[H,\alpha:C(X)\to\mathcal{B}(H),F]:=[H,\alpha\circ\psi^{*}:C(Y)\to\mathcal{B}(H),F]\in\mathrm{K}_{*}^{{\operatorname{an}}}(Y)\,.

A similar formula works in the unbounded picture, considering a suitable dense subalgebra
\mathcal{A}(X)
of
C(X)
.
Consider now
H^{\prime}_{X}:=L^{2}(X^{{\rm reg}},\Lambda^{*}(X^{{\rm reg}}),\phi^{*}g_{Y})
and the class

[H^{\prime}_{X},\mathcal{M}_{X}:\mathcal{A}(X)\to\mathcal{B}(H^{\prime}_{X}),D^{\prime}_{X}]\in\mathrm{K}_{*}^{{\operatorname{an}}}(X)

with
D^{\prime}_{X}
the signature operator associated to

\phi^{*}g_{Y}
,
\mathcal{A}(X)
the dense subalgebra of Lipschitz functions
on
X
and
\mathcal{M}_{X}
equal to the multiplication operator.
By wedge-metric invariance

[H^{\prime}_{X},\mathcal{M}_{X}:\mathcal{A}(X)\to\mathcal{B}(H^{\prime}_{X}),D^{\prime}_{X}]=[D_{X}^{\mathrm{sign}}]\in\mathrm{K}_{*}^{{\operatorname{an}}}(X)

On the other hand, let now
H_{Y}
be the space of

L^{2}
-forms on
Y
associated to
g_{Y}
. Pull-back by
\phi
defines a unitary isomorphism
U:H_{Y}\to H^{\prime}_{X}

such that
U^{-1}\circ D^{\prime}_{X}\circ U=D_{Y}^{\mathrm{sign}}
or, equivalently,
U\circ D_{Y}^{\mathrm{sign}}\circ U^{-1}=D^{\prime}_{X}
.
Thus, on the one hand,

\phi_{*}[D_{X}^{\mathrm{sign}}]=\phi_{*}[H^{\prime}_{X},\mathcal{M}_{X}:\mathcal{A}(X)\to\mathcal{B}(H^{\prime}_{X}),D^{\prime}_{X}]=[H^{\prime}_{X},\mathcal{M}_{X}\circ\phi^{*}:\mathcal{A}(Y)\to\mathcal{B}(H^{\prime}_{X}),D^{\prime}_{X}]

and, on the other hand,

[D_{Y}^{\mathrm{sign}}]=[H_{Y},\mathcal{M}_{Y}:\mathcal{A}(Y)\to\mathcal{B}(H_{Y}),D_{Y}^{\mathrm{sign}}].

We set
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}:=\mathcal{M}_{X}\circ\phi^{*}
; we want to show that

[H^{\prime}_{X},\mathcal{M}^{\prime}:\mathcal{A}(Y)\to\mathcal{B}(H^{\prime}_{X}),D^{\prime}_{X}]=[H_{Y},\mathcal{M}_{Y}:\mathcal{A}(Y)\to\mathcal{B}(H_{Y}),D_{Y}^{\mathrm{sign}}].

But for
\omega\in H_{Y}
we have

\mathcal{M}^{\prime}(f)(U\omega)=\phi^{*}(f)(\phi^{*}\omega)=\phi^{*}(f\omega)=U(\mathcal{M}_{Y}(f)(\omega)).

Together with our remarks so far, this means that the 2 cycles

(H^{\prime}_{X},\mathcal{M}^{\prime}:\mathcal{A}(Y)\to\mathcal{B}(H^{\prime}_{X}),D^{\prime}_{X})\;\;\text{and}\;\;(H_{Y},\mathcal{M}_{Y}:\mathcal{A}(Y)\to\mathcal{B}(H_{Y}),D_{Y}^{\mathrm{sign}})

are unitarily equivalent, and so define the same
K
-theory class, which is what we wanted to show.
∎

Remark 4.2
.

For a general wedge Clifford module
(g_{M},E\longrightarrow M,g_{E},\nabla^{E}),
the resulting K-homology class may well depend on the metrics involved.
The reason that the argument above does not apply is that, while a homotopy of metrics lifts to a homotopy of the corresponding Dirac-type operators, this homotopy might not take place within Fredholm operators. For the signature operator the analytic Witt condition is independent of the metric but for example the dimension of the space of harmonic spinors on a surface of genus greater than two depends on the metric
[
Hit74
, Theorem 2.6]
.

4.2.
Witt bordism invariance

The following proposition, for smooth manifolds, is Proposition 4.1 in
[
PRW95
]
(cf.
[
BDT89
, Propositions 4.4 & 5.4]
). Our proof follows
[
Hig89
, §5]
(cf.
[
HR00
, Proposition 11.2.15]
). A different approach for smooth manifolds can be found in
[
MP92
]
.

Proposition 4.3

(The boundary of Dirac is the Dirac of the boundary)
.

Let
W
be a smoothly stratified space with boundary
X=\partial W,
and denote their resolutions by
N=\mathrm{res}(W),
and
M=\mathrm{res}(X)=\partial N.

Let
(g_{N},E\longrightarrow N,g_{E},\nabla^{E})
be a wedge Clifford module on
N
whose associated Dirac-type operator
\eth_{N,VAPS}^{E}
satisfies the analytic Witt condition.
If
W
is even-dimensional, assume that
E
is graded with grading operator
\gamma.
Let
\nu
be a normal vector field to
M
and define a
wedge Clifford module
(g_{N}|_{M},\partial E\longrightarrow M,g_{E}|_{\partial E},\nabla^{E}|_{\partial E})
on
M
as follows:

(1)

If
W
is odd-dimensional,
\partial E=E|_{M},
graded by Clifford multiplication by
i\nu;

(2)

if
W
is even-dimensional,
\partial E
is the
+1
eigenspace of the involution
i\nu\gamma.

Then the wedge Dirac-type operator associated to this Clifford module,
\eth_{M,VAPS}^{\partial E}
satisfies the analytic Witt condition and the boundary map in K-homology,
\partial:\mathrm{K}^{\operatorname{an}}_{\dim W}(W,\partial W)\longrightarrow\mathrm{K}^{\operatorname{an}}_{\dim\partial W}(\partial W),
satisfies

\partial[\eth_{N,VAPS}^{E}]=[\eth_{M,VAPS}^{\partial E}].

Proof.

It is pointed out in
[
Hig89
, §5]
that if
A
is a
C^{*}
-algebra and
J
an ideal in
A,
so that we have a short exact sequence

0\longrightarrow J\longrightarrow A\longrightarrow A\diagup J\longrightarrow 0,

then the boundary map
\partial:\mathrm{KK}^{j}(J,\mathbb{C})\longrightarrow\mathrm{KK}^{j-1}(A\diagup J,\mathbb{C})
factors as

(4.1)

\mathrm{KK}^{j}(J,\mathbb{C})\longrightarrow\mathrm{KK}^{j}(C_{0}((0,1))\otimes A\diagup J,\mathbb{C})\longrightarrow\mathrm{KK}^{j-1}(A\diagup J,\mathbb{C})

where the first map is defined using the mapping cone associated to the short exact sequence and the second map is the inverse of the
Kasparov product with the class

[\eth_{(0,1)}]\in\mathrm{KK}^{1}(C_{0}(0,1),\mathbb{C})=\mathrm{K}^{\operatorname{an}}_{1}((0,1))

of the Dirac operator on the positively oriented open unit interval.

We wish to apply this with
A=C_{0}(W),

J=\{f\in C_{0}(W):f|_{X}=0\},
so that
A\diagup J=C_{0}(X).

Naturality of the boundary map allows us to replace
W
with a collar neighborhood of
X
so instead we take
A=C_{0}([0,1)\times X)
and

J=C_{0}((0,1)\times X).

Arguing as in the proof of
[
Hig89
, Theorem 5.1]
this has the happy consequence that the first map in (
4.1
) is the identity map
and hence the boundary map in K-homology is the inverse of the Kasparov product with
[\eth_{(0,1)}].
Thus we can conclude the proof if we can show that

[\eth_{N,VAPS}^{E}]=[\eth_{(0,1)}]\otimes[\eth_{M,VAPS}^{\partial E}]\in\mathrm{K}^{\operatorname{an}}_{\dim W}([0,1)\times X,\{0\}\times X).

This can be checked using the fact (e.g., from
[
BJ83
, (1)]
) that the Kasparov product of
[\eth_{(0,1)}]
and
[\eth_{M,VAPS}^{\partial E}]
is represented by
\eth_{(0,1)}\hat{\otimes}I+I\hat{\otimes}\eth_{M,VAPS}^{\partial E}.
This is also a particular case of Theorem
5.16
below.
∎

In the non-singular situation, the following proposition has been established in
[
PRW95
, pg. 290]
,
[
RW06
, Theorem 2]
.
We note that certain bordism invariance results for the
signature operator
{\operatorname{K}}
-homology class on Witt spaces have also been
obtained by Hilsum in
[
Hil14
, Section 3]
.

We use the notation
[D_{W,\partial W}^{\mathrm{sign}}]
to denote the class induced by the signature operator of a wedge metric on an oriented Witt space with boundary in the K-homology group of
W
relative to
\partial W.

Proposition 4.4
.

(1)

If
W
is an oriented smoothly stratified Witt space with boundary then the boundary map in K-homology,
\partial:\mathrm{K}^{\operatorname{an}}_{\dim W}(W,\partial W)\longrightarrow\mathrm{K}^{\operatorname{an}}_{\dim\partial W}(\partial W),
satisfies

\partial[D_{W,\partial W}^{\mathrm{sign}}]=k[D_{\partial W}^{\mathrm{sign}}]\quad\text{ with }\quad k=\begin{cases}1&\text{ if $\dim W$ is even}\\
2&\text{ if $\dim W$ is odd}\end{cases}

(2)

Let
X
be a compact oriented smoothly stratified Witt space,

\mathscr{Y}
any finite CW complex,
and let
f:X\to\mathscr{Y}
be a continuous map.
Then
f_{*}[D_{X}^{\mathrm{sign}}]\in\mathrm{K}_{\dim\mathscr{Y}}(\mathscr{Y})
is a Witt bordism invariant of the pair
(X,f).

Proof.

(1) Denote the resolution of
W
by
N=\mathrm{res}(W)
and that of
\partial W
by
M=\mathrm{res}(\partial W)=\partial N.

Since the signature operator of a wedge metric on
W
is a Dirac-type operator we know from Proposition
4.3
that
\partial[D_{W}^{\mathrm{sign}}]
is the K-homology class of a Dirac-type operator
\eth
on
\partial W.
Without loss of generality we may assume that the wedge metric on
N
is collared near
\partial N,
i.e., that there is a collar neighborhood of
N
which is isometric to
[0,1)\times M
for some wedge metric on
M,
and we may restrict attention to this neighborhood. Rosenberg and Weinberger computed the decomposition of the signature operator on a product of closed manifolds in
[
RW06
, Lemma 6]
but, as the computation only involves the Clifford algebras and gradings, it applies to
[0,1)\times M,
and shows that, in K-homology,

[D_{[0,1)\times\partial W}^{\mathrm{sign}}]=k\;[D_{[0,1)}^{\mathrm{sign}}]\otimes[D_{\partial W}^{\mathrm{sign}}]\quad\text{ with }\quad k=\begin{cases}1&\text{ if $\dim W$ is even}\\
2&\text{ if $\dim W$ is odd}\end{cases}

Finally we know from the proof of Proposition
4.3
that the boundary map in K-homology for a product is the inverse of the Kasparov
product by the Dirac operator on the interval, so the result follows.

(2)
Let
n=\dim X.

Suppose that
f:X\longrightarrow\mathscr{Y}
is Witt nullbordant.
Thus, there is a compact smoothly stratified oriented Witt space
W

with boundary
\partial W=X
and a continuous map
F:W\to\mathscr{Y}

that extends
f
. Let
i:X\hookrightarrow W
denote the
inclusion of the boundary and
consider the commutative diagram

\textstyle{{\operatorname{K}}_{n+1}(W,X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\partial_{*}}
\textstyle{{\operatorname{K}}_{n}^{{\operatorname{an}}}(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{*}}
\scriptstyle{i_{*}}
\textstyle{{\operatorname{K}}_{n}^{{\operatorname{an}}}(W)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{F_{*}}
\textstyle{{\operatorname{K}}_{n}^{{\operatorname{an}}}(\mathscr{Y}),}

whose top row is exact.
If
n
is even, then from (1) we know
\partial[D_{W,X}^{\mathrm{sign}}]=[D_{X}^{\mathrm{sign}}]
and thus

f_{*}[D_{X}^{\mathrm{sign}}]=F_{*}i_{*}[D_{X}^{\mathrm{sign}}]=F_{*}i_{*}\partial[D_{W,X}^{\mathrm{sign}}]=0.

If
n
is odd, then (1) asserts that
\partial[D_{W,X}^{\mathrm{sign}}]=2[D_{X}^{\mathrm{sign}}]
.
Therefore,
f_{*}[D_{X}^{\mathrm{sign}}]
is either zero or has order
2
:

2f_{*}[D_{X}^{\mathrm{sign}}]=F_{*}i_{*}(2[D_{X}^{\mathrm{sign}}])=F_{*}i_{*}\partial[D_{W,X}^{\mathrm{sign}}]=0\in{\operatorname{K}}_{n}^{{\operatorname{an}}}(\mathscr{Y}).

In either case,
f_{*}[D_{X}^{\mathrm{sign}}]
vanishes in the localization
{\operatorname{K}}_{n}^{{\operatorname{an}}}(\mathscr{Y})[\mbox{$\frac{1}{2}$}]
. However
as in
[
RW06
, Theorem 2]
it is possible to use arguments from
[
PRW95
, §4]
to avoid having to invert
2.

Specifically, Pedersen, Roe, and Weinberger point out that on a smooth manifold with boundary equipped with a unit vector field
\nu
which is normal to the boundary (but defined on all of the manifold) the operator of right Clifford multiplication by
i\nu
is an involution and the restriction of the signature operator
to either of its eigenspaces produces an operator whose boundary is the signature operator of the boundary on the nose. The argument above then establishes that
f_{*}[D_{X}^{\mathrm{sign}}]=0\in{\operatorname{K}}_{n}^{{\operatorname{an}}}(\mathscr{Y}).

A smooth manifold with boundary admits such a vector field if and only if its Euler characteristic vanishes. Rosenberg and Weinberger explain how to reduce to this case by making modifications (either a connected sum or punching out a small disk) away from the boundary.

To apply these arguments to our situation first note that if two bundles are isomorphic and one has a nowhere vanishing section then so does the other.
If
N
is the resolution of
W,

N=\mathrm{res}(W),
then we may ‘round out the corners’ and homotope
N
to a smooth manifold with boundary
N^{\prime}.

If
N
has vanishing Euler characteristic then
N^{\prime}
has a smooth nowhere vanishing vector field normal to the boundary. Since the tangent bundle of
N
is
isomorphic to the pull-back of the tangent bundle of
N^{\prime},

N
also has a nowhere vanishing vector field which we can assume (by how we round out the corners) is normal to the boundary hypersurfaces of
N
that correspond to the boundary of
W.
Next, since the wedge tangent bundle of
N
is isomorphic (albeit not naturally isomorphic) to the ordinary tangent bundle, we see that there is a nowhere vanishing wedge vector field on
N,
normal to the lift of the boundary of
W,
which we can normalize to have everywhere unit length. Applying the Pedersen, Roe, and Weinberger argument as done in
[
RW06
, Theorem 2]
then establishes the result.
∎

Recall from §
2.5
that
\Omega^{{\operatorname{Witt}}}_{n}
denotes the bordism
theory based on oriented PL Witt pseudomanifolds and
\Omega^{{\operatorname{Witt}},\infty}_{n}
denotes the corresponding theory based on oriented smoothly stratified Witt pseudomanifolds.
The proposition implies that the assignment

(4.2)

[f:W^{n}\to X]\ \mapsto f_{*}[D_{W}]

constitutes a well defined homomorphism

\Omega^{{\operatorname{Witt}},\infty}_{n}(X)\longrightarrow{\operatorname{K}}^{\operatorname{an}}_{n}(X).

Since bordism of PL stratified Witt spaces is
isomorphic to bordism of smoothly stratified Witt spaces
(Proposition
4.5
), we may also
regard this as a homomorphism

\Omega^{{\operatorname{Witt}}}_{n}(X)\longrightarrow{\operatorname{K}}^{\operatorname{an}}_{n}(X).

This map is a natural transformation of homotopy functors, but
not of homology theories (Rosenberg-Weinberger

[
RW06
, Remark 4]
).
The modified map

(4.3)

\begin{gathered}\theta:\Omega^{{\operatorname{Witt}},\infty}_{n}(X)[\mbox{$\frac{1}{2}$}]\longrightarrow{\operatorname{K}}^{\operatorname{an}}_{n}(X)[\mbox{$\frac{1}{2}$}],\\
\theta([f:W^{n}\to X]\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}r):=r2^{-\lfloor n/2\rfloor}f_{*}[D_{W}^{\mathrm{sign}}]=rf_{*}(\mathrm{sign}_{K}(W)),\end{gathered}

is
a natural transformation of homology theories, since
the powers of
2
correctly absorb the behavior of the
connecting homomorphism as described in Proposition
4.4
(
1
).

4.3.
Witt bordism: smooth versus PL

In several places, particularly in Section
7

where we consider a natural transformation from Witt bordism to
analytic
{\operatorname{K}}
-homology via the signature operator,
we need to identify Siegel’s bordism theory of
PL Witt spaces with bordism of smoothly stratified Witt
spaces. Let us discuss this identification in more detail.
Recall that
\Omega^{\operatorname{Witt}}_{*}(-)
denotes the bordism theory of PL Witt spaces
as introduced in
[
Sie83
]
. This functor is well-known to be
a homology theory.
Let
\Omega^{{\operatorname{Witt}},\infty}_{*}(-)
denote the bordism theory based on
cycles given by continuous maps on closed
smoothly stratified Witt spaces.
This functor is a homology theory as well, since transversality is
available for smoothly stratified spaces.

Proposition 4.5
.

Triangulation induces a natural equivalence of homology theories

t:\Omega^{{\operatorname{Witt}},\infty}_{*}(-)\longrightarrow\Omega^{\operatorname{Witt}}_{*}(-).

Proof.

Let
\mathscr{Y}
be a CW complex and
let
f:X^{n}\to\mathscr{Y}
be a continuous map on a closed smoothly
stratified Witt space
X
. By Goresky
[
Gor78
]
,
Thom-Mather pseudomanifolds can be triangulated compatibly with
the stratification. The triangulation can be taken to be a smooth
embedding on the interiors of simplices. Choose a Goresky-triangulation
of
X
. This choice yields a PL pseudomanifold
\overline{X}
with the same
underlying topological space as
X
. It is not clear that different
choices of Goresky-triangulations yield PL-isomorphic PL pseudomanifolds.
Nevertheless, we claim that
(f:X\to\mathscr{Y})\mapsto(f:\overline{X}\to\mathscr{Y})

induces a well-defined
homomorphism
t:\Omega^{{\operatorname{Witt}},\infty}_{*}(\mathscr{Y})\to\Omega^{\operatorname{Witt}}_{*}(\mathscr{Y})
.
The reason is indicated by Goresky and MacPherson in Section 5.3 of

[
GM80
]
: Goresky’s techniques imply that any two
Goresky-triangulations of
X
are concordant.
This yields a triangulation of the cylinder

X\times[0,1]
, which is then a PL bordism between the two triangulations
of
X
. The map
f
extends continuously over the cylinder by

f\times\operatorname{id}_{[0,1]}
.
If
F:W\to\mathscr{Y}
is a smoothly stratified Witt nullbordism for

f:X=\partial W\to\mathscr{Y}
, then we may Goresky-triangulate
W
to obtain
a PL Witt nullbordism
F:\overline{W}\to\mathscr{Y}
for
f:\overline{X}\to\mathscr{Y}
,
where
\overline{X}
is chosen to be the restriction of the Goresky-triangulation
of
\overline{W}
to the boundary.
Hence we obtain a well-defined map
t
and this map is a homomorphism.
It is natural in
\mathscr{Y}
, since maps induced by
g:\mathscr{Y}\to\mathscr{Y}^{\prime}
are just given
by composing
f
and
g
.
For
\mathscr{Y}
a point, the map
t
was already considered by Zentarra in
[
Zen19
]
,
who proved that it is an isomorphism over a point.
(The map is surjective, since Siegel’s generators of

\Omega^{\operatorname{Witt}}_{4k}(\operatorname{pt})
are given by Whitehead-triangulating certain compact smooth
