these requirements with respect to the
p_{12}
-horizontal edge vector fields and boundary hypersurfaces, so this inclusion is clear.

Finally to establish (iii) we can appeal to the computation done in
[
KvS20
, Lemma 17]
and the proof of

[
KvS20
, Theorem 23]
to see that it suffices to show that there exists a constant
C>0
such that for any
\xi
in the domain of

\eth^{\cal E_{12}}_{X_{1}/X_{2}},

\lVert\;|\nabla^{X_{1}/X_{2}}\xi|_{g_{X_{1}/X_{2}}}\rVert_{L^{2}}\leq C(\lVert\xi\rVert_{L^{2}}+\lVert\eth^{E_{12}}_{X_{1}/X_{2}}\xi\rVert_{L^{2}}).

While the analogous inequality holds for edge Sobolev spaces (by, e.g., the bounded geometry of edge metrics) this inequality does not hold in general
for elliptic wedge differential operators. This is why in Step 1 we arranged for the VAPS domain of each operator in the family
\eth^{E_{12}}_{X_{1}/X_{2}}
is
\rho H^{1}_{e}(\hat{p}_{12}^{-1}(\zeta);E)
as this inequality does hold for elements in this space.

The only difference when either
\dim X_{1}/X_{2}
or
\dim X_{2}/X_{3}
is odd is the treatment of the gradings. This aspect can be handled just as when the underlying spaces are smooth and the fiber bundles are trivial. We refer to
[
BMvS16
, Examples 2.38-2.40]
and especially
[
Wah10
]
for a thorough discussion.
∎

Corollary 5.17
.

Given fiber bundles of smoothly stratified spaces as in (
5.10
), if the fibers of
p_{12}
and
p_{23}
are smooth oriented Witt pseudomanifolds then so are the fibers of
p_{13}
and the associated analytic signature classes satisfy

[D^{\mathrm{sign}}_{X_{1}/X_{3}}]=\ell\big([D^{\mathrm{sign}}_{X_{1}/X_{2}}]\otimes[D^{\mathrm{sign}}_{X_{2}/X_{3}}]\big)\text{ in }\mathrm{KK}^{*}(C_{0}(X_{1}),C_{0}(X_{3})),\\
\text{ with }\ell=\begin{cases}2&\text{ if }\dim X_{1}/X_{2}\text{ and }\dim X_{2}/X_{3}\text{ are odd }\\
1&\text{ otherwise }\end{cases}

Correspondingly, we have

\Sigma(p_{13})=\Sigma(p_{12})\otimes\Sigma(p_{23})\text{ in }\mathrm{KK}^{*}(C_{0}(X_{1}),C_{0}(X_{3}))[\tfrac{1}{2}].

Proof.

The fact that the fibers of
p_{13}
satisfy the topological Witt condition reduces to the fact that the product of Witt spaces is a Witt space, which is proved, e.g., in Proposition 9.1.25 of
[
Fri20
]
.

The theorem establishes that the product of
[D^{\mathrm{sign}}_{X_{1}/X_{2}}]
and
[D^{\mathrm{sign}}_{X_{2}/X_{3}}]
is the KK-class of a family of Dirac-type operators associated to
p_{13},
and the construction above specializes to show that this operator is
d+\delta
acting on the vertical wedge differential forms, so it is only necessary to work out the grading. That this results in the formula in the statement of the corollary is explained in
[
RW06
, Lemma 6]
(see also
[
Wah10
]
and
[
Ebe25
, Proposition 2.29]
).

For the analytic transfer classes, if we define

i=\dim X_{1}-\dim X_{2},\quad j=\dim X_{2}-\dim X_{3}

then
\Sigma(p_{13})
is equal to

2^{-\lfloor(i+j)/2\rfloor}[D^{\mathrm{sign}}_{X_{1}/X_{3}}]

so it suffices to note that

\ell\big(2^{-\lfloor(i+j)/2\rfloor}\big)=2^{-\lfloor i/2\rfloor}2^{-\lfloor j/2\rfloor},\text{ with }\ell=\begin{cases}2&\text{ if }i\text{ and }j\text{ are odd }\\
1&\text{ otherwise }\end{cases}

∎

It is instructive to consider the case of Theorem

5.15
where
p
is the factor projection
of a product bundle, which generalizes

[
RW06
, Lemma 6, p. 51]
from the manifold case to
singular spaces.

Corollary 5.18
.

Let
X^{n}
and
Y^{m}
be closed smoothly stratified Witt spaces.
Then the product
X\times Y
is a closed smoothly stratified Witt space, and
the
{\operatorname{K}}
-homology class of the signature operator on
X\times Y
satisfies

[D^{\mathrm{sign}}_{X\times Y}]=\ell\big([D^{\mathrm{sign}}_{X}]\boxtimes[D^{\mathrm{sign}}_{Y}]\big)\text{ in }\mathrm{K}_{n+m}^{\mathrm{an}}(X\times Y),\\
\text{ with }\ell=\begin{cases}2&\text{ if }\dim X\text{ and }\dim Y\text{ are odd }\\
1&\text{ otherwise }\end{cases}

where
\boxtimes
denotes the external Kasparov product

{\operatorname{K}}^{\operatorname{an}}_{n}(X)\otimes{\operatorname{K}}^{\operatorname{an}}_{m}(Y)\to{\operatorname{K}}^{\operatorname{an}}_{n+m}(X\times Y)
.
Correspondingly we have

\mathrm{sign}_{K}(X\times Y)=\mathrm{sign}_{K}(X)\boxtimes\mathrm{sign}_{K}(Y)\text{ in }\mathrm{K}_{n+m}^{\mathrm{an}}(X\times Y)[\tfrac{1}{2}].

Proof.

Apply the theorem to the case in which
X_{1}=X\times Y,

X_{2}=Y,

X_{3}=\mathrm{pt}
and
p_{12}
is the natural projection
X\times Y\longrightarrow Y.

∎

Another interesting case concerns spin Dirac operators. If the vertical Clifford structures on
p_{12}
and
p_{23}
correspond to spin structure on their fibers, then the induced vertical Clifford structure on
p_{13}
does as well and we have the following result.

Corollary 5.19
.

Given fiber bundles of smoothly stratified spaces as in (
5.10
), whose vertical wedge tangent bundles are spin, equipped with vertical wedge metrics such that the associated vertical families of spin Dirac operators satisfy the analytic Witt condition, the associated KK-classes satisfy

[D^{\mathrm{spin}}_{X_{1}/X_{3}}]=[D^{\mathrm{spin}}_{X_{1}/X_{2}}]\otimes[D^{\mathrm{spin}}_{X_{2}/X_{3}}]\in\mathrm{KK}^{\dim X_{1}/X_{3}}(C_{0}(X_{1}),C_{0}(X_{3})).

Remark 5.20
.

The corollary applies, for example, if the fibers of
p_{12},

p_{23},
and
p_{13}
all satisfy the psc-Witt condition (described after Corollary
5.12
).

Notice that, unlike the topological Witt condition, assuming that the fibers of
p_{12}
and
p_{23}
satisfy this condition does not imply that the fibers of
p_{13}
will satisfy the psc-Witt condition.
Indeed, it is not difficult to see that the product of two cones satisfying the psc-Witt condition need not satisfy the psc-Witt condition.

5.4.
Base change

In this subsection we show that the Gysin homomorphism associated to a fiber bundle is compatible with pull-back along proper smooth maps of stratified spaces.

Specifically suppose that
p:X\longrightarrow Y
is a smooth oriented fiber bundle of stratified spaces, whose typical fiber
Z
is a Witt pseudomanifold,
W
is another smoothly stratified space and
f:W\longrightarrow Y
is a proper smooth stratified map, and then consider the Cartesian diagram

\textstyle{f^{*}X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g}
\scriptstyle{q}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p}
\textstyle{W\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{Y}

with
f^{*}X\xrightarrow{\phantom{x}q\phantom{x}}W
the pull-back bundle.
We assume that
X
is endowed with a vertical totally geodesic wedge metric and then we endow
f^{*}X
with the pull-back metric along
g.

We have two bivariant classes

[D_{X/Y}^{\mathrm{sign}}]\in\mathrm{KK}(C_{0}(X),C_{0}(Y)),\,\quad[D_{f^{*}X/W}^{\mathrm{sign}}]\in\mathrm{KK}(C_{0}(f^{*}X),C_{0}(W)),

where
D_{X/Y}^{\mathrm{sign}}
denotes the vertical family of signature operators endowed with their VAPS domain, and similarly for
D_{f^{*}X/W}^{\mathrm{sign}},

and by Kasparov multiplication they define two homomorphisms

[D_{X/Y}^{\mathrm{sign}}]\otimes\cdot:\mathrm{K}_{*}^{\mathrm{an}}(Y)\longrightarrow\mathrm{K}_{*}^{\mathrm{an}}(X),\quad[D_{f^{*}X/W}^{\mathrm{sign}}]\otimes\cdot:\mathrm{K}_{*}^{\mathrm{an}}(W)\longrightarrow\mathrm{K}_{*}^{\mathrm{an}}(f^{*}X).

We will deduce base change from the following:

Theorem 5.21
.

If
f,

g,

p,
and
q
are above then the following holds

[D_{X/Y}^{\mathrm{sign}}]\otimes f_{*}\alpha=g_{*}([D_{f^{*}X/W}^{\mathrm{sign}}]\otimes\alpha),\quad\forall\alpha\in\mathrm{K}_{*}^{\mathrm{an}}(W).

Proof.

Since
f
is proper, it defines
f^{*}:C_{0}(Y)\to C_{0}(W)
, a
C^{*}
-algebra homomorphism, and

f_{*}(\alpha)=[f^{*}]\otimes\alpha,\quad\text{with}\quad[f^{*}]\in\mathrm{KK}(C_{0}(Y),C_{0}(W))

and, since
g
is also proper, similarly for
g_{*}
, with

[g^{*}]\in\mathrm{KK}(C_{0}(X),C_{0}(f^{*}X))
. Here we are using the well known fact that if
A
and
B
are two
C^{*}
-algebras
and
\phi:A\to B
is a homomorphism then there is a natural KK-class

[\phi]:=[B,\phi:A\to B,0]\in\mathrm{KK}(A,B)

where we regard
B
as a
B
-Hilbert module with the
B
-valued inner product equal to
\langle b,b^{\prime}\rangle:=(b^{\prime})^{*}b
. This class
[\phi]\in\mathrm{KK}(A,B)
has the property that

\phi^{*}:\mathrm{KK}(B,D)\to\mathrm{KK}(A,D)
is given by
[\phi]\otimes(-):\mathrm{KK}(B,D)\to\mathrm{KK}(A,D)
for any
C^{*}
-algebra
D
.

Hence, by associativity of the Kasparov product, we see that the theorem is equivalent to the
equality

[D_{X/Y}^{\mathrm{sign}}]\otimes[f^{*}]=[g^{*}]\otimes[D_{f^{*}X/W}^{\mathrm{sign}}]\quad\text{in}\quad\mathrm{KK}(C_{0}(X),C_{0}(W))\,.

In general if

{\bf x}=[E_{1},\phi_{1}:A\to\mathbb{B}(E_{1}),F_{1}]\in\mathrm{KK}(A,D)

and
if
\lambda:D\to B
is a
C^{*}
-homomorphism and

{\bf y}=[B,\lambda:D\to B,0]\in\mathrm{KK}(D,B)

then

{\bf x}\otimes{\bf y}=[E=E_{1}\otimes_{\lambda}B,\phi_{1}\otimes_{\lambda}{\rm Id},F\otimes{\rm Id}]\in\mathrm{KK}(A,B).

One should observe that
\lambda
induces a group homomorphism:

\lambda_{*}:\mathrm{KK}(-,D)\to\mathrm{KK}(-,B)

and

{\bf x}\otimes{\bf y}=\lambda_{*}{\bf x}.

Similarly if

{\bf x}=[D,\mu:A\to D,0]\in\mathrm{KK}(A,D)

and

{\bf y}=[E_{2},\phi_{2}:D\to\mathbb{B}(E_{2}),F_{2}]\in\mathrm{KK}(D,B)

then

{\bf x}\otimes{\bf y}=[E=D\otimes_{\phi_{2}}E_{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Phi}}{{\simeq}}E_{2},f\otimes_{\phi_{2}}{\rm Id},\Phi^{*}F_{2}\Phi]\in\mathrm{KK}(A,B).

Notice that
\mu
induces

\mu^{*}:\mathrm{KK}(D,-)\to\mathrm{KK}(A,-)

and it holds that

\mu^{*}{\bf y}={\bf x}\otimes{\bf y}\,.

We go back to our specific situation and write down the relevant cycles.

Let
E
be the bundle of exterior powers of wedge vertical differential forms on
X,

E=\Lambda^{*}({}^{w}T^{*}X/Y).

We denote by
L^{2}(X/Y;E)
the bundle of Hilbert spaces over
Y
with fiber at
y\in Y
given by

L^{2}(X_{y},E_{y})
with
X_{y}=p^{-1}(y)
and
E_{y}
the restriction of
E
to
X_{y}
.
Then

[D_{X/Y}^{\mathrm{sign}}]:=[H_{X/Y}:=C_{0}(Y,L^{2}(X/Y;E)),\phi:C_{0}(X)\to\mathbb{B}(H_{X/Y}),D_{X/Y}^{\mathrm{sign}}]\\
\in\mathrm{KK}(C_{0}(X),C_{0}(Y))

where
C_{0}(Y,L^{2}(X/Y;E))
denotes continuous sections vanishing at infinity of the bundle of Hilbert spaces

L^{2}(X/Y;E).

We also have
[f^{*}]\in\mathrm{KK}(C_{0}(Y),C_{0}(W))
given by

[C_{0}(W),f^{*}:C_{0}(Y)\to C_{0}(W),0]\in\mathrm{KK}(C_{0}(Y),C_{0}(W)).

Next we write the elements appearing on the right hand side of the base change formula.
These are

[g^{*}]=[C_{0}(f^{*}X),g^{*}:C_{0}(X)\to C_{0}(f^{*}X),0]\in\mathrm{KK}(C_{0}(X),C_{0}(f^{*}X)),

whereas
[D_{f^{*}X/W}^{\mathrm{sign}}]
is given by

[H_{f^{*}X/W}:=C_{0}(W,L^{2}(f^{*}X/W,g^{*}E)),\tilde{\phi}:C_{0}(f^{*}X)\to\mathbb{B}(H_{f^{*}X/W}),D_{f^{*}X/W}^{\mathrm{sign}}]\\
\in\mathrm{KK}(C_{0}(f^{*}X),C_{0}(W)).

Lemma 5.22
.

There is an isomorphism

L^{2}(f^{*}X/W,g^{*}E)=f^{*}L^{2}(X/Y;E)

of bundles of Hilbert spaces over
W
.

Proof.

By our definition of fiber bundle of smoothly stratified spaces, we know that there is an open cover of
Y,

\{U_{i}\}_{i\in\cal A},
together with corresponding local trivializations of
p:X\to Y
of the form

\textstyle{p^{-1}(U_{i})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varphi_{i}}
\scriptstyle{p}
\textstyle{Z\times U_{i}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{U_{i}}

where the right arrow is the projection onto the right factor and where
\varphi_{i}
is a stratified diffeomorphism.
Whenever
i,j\in\cal A
are such that
U_{ij}:=U_{i}\cap\cal U_{j}\neq\emptyset
the map

Z\times U_{ij}\xrightarrow{\phantom{x}\varphi_{j}\circ\varphi_{i}^{-1}\phantom{x}}Z\times U_{ij}

necessarily has the form
(z,u)\mapsto(\rho_{ij}(u)(z),u)
with transition functions
\rho_{ij},
a family of stratified diffeomorphisms of
Z
parametrized by points of
U_{ij}.
(This family is smooth in the sense that they fit together into the smooth map
\varphi_{j}\circ\varphi_{i}^{-1}.
)
If
i,j,k\in\cal A
are such that
\cal U_{i}\cap\cal U_{j}\cap\cal U_{k}\neq\emptyset
then, on this set, the transition functions satisfy the cocycle condition
\rho_{ij}=\rho_{kj}\circ\rho_{ij}.

At a point
u\in U_{ij},
the stratified diffeomorphism

\rho_{ij}(u):Z\to Z
induces an isomorphism of Hilbert spaces

\rho^{*}_{ij}(u):L^{2}(Z)\to L^{2}(Z)
by pulling back an
L^{2}
-function on
Z

via
\rho_{ij}(u)
, i.e.

(\rho^{*}_{ij}(u))(h)=h\circ(\rho_{ij}(u))

for
h\in L^{2}(Z)
.
This yields a system

\{\rho^{*}_{ij}:U_{ij}\to\mathrm{GL}(L^{2}(Z))\}
of transition functions satisfying
the cocycle condition. This system is the system of transition functions
associated to the bundle
L^{2}(X/Y)
of Hilbert spaces.

The pulled-back smooth stratified fiber bundle
q:f^{*}X\to W
is trivialized over the open cover
\{V_{i}=f^{-1}(U_{i})\}_{i\in\cal A}
of
W,
with local trivializations

\textstyle{q^{-1}(V_{i})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\psi_{i}:=(\pi_{L}\circ\varphi_{i}\circ g,q)}
\scriptstyle{p}
\textstyle{Z\times V_{i}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{V_{i}}

where
\pi_{L}:Z\times U_{i}\longrightarrow Z
is the projection onto the left factor.
Whenever
i,j\in\cal A
are such that
V_{ij}:=V_{i}\cap V_{j}\neq\emptyset
the map

Z\times V_{ij}\xrightarrow{\phantom{x}\psi_{j}\circ\psi_{i}^{-1}\phantom{x}}Z\times V_{ij}

is given by
(z,u)\mapsto(\rho_{ij}(f(u))(z),u),
i.e., the transition functions of
q
are
\tau_{ij}=\rho_{ij}\circ f.

At a point
v\in V_{ij},
the stratified diffeomorphism

\tau_{ij}(v):Z\to Z
induces an isomorphism of Hilbert spaces

\tau^{*}_{ij}(v):L^{2}(Z)\to L^{2}(Z)
by pulling back an
L^{2}
-function on
Z

via
\tau_{ij}(v)
, i.e.

(\tau^{*}_{ij}(v))(h)=h\circ(\tau_{ij}(v))

for
h\in L^{2}(Z)
.
This yields a system

\{\tau^{*}_{ij}:V_{ij}\to\mathrm{GL}(L^{2}(Z))\}
of transition functions satisfying
the cocycle condition which is the system of transition functions
for the Hilbert space bundle
L^{2}(f^{*}X/W)
.
On the other hand,
the transition functions of the pullback Hilbert space bundle

f^{*}L^{2}(X/Y)
over
W
are given by composing the transition
functions of
L^{2}(X/Y)
with
f
, that is,

f^{*}L^{2}(X/Y)
has transition functions

\sigma_{ij}:V_{ij}\to\mathrm{GL}(L^{2}(Z))
,

\sigma_{ij}(v)=\rho^{*}_{ij}\circ f
.
Now, on a function
h\in L^{2}(Z),

\sigma_{ij}(v)(h)=\rho^{*}_{ij}(f(v))(h)=h\circ(\rho_{ij}(f(v)))=h\circ(\tau_{ij}(v))=(\tau^{*}_{ij}(v))(h).

This shows that
f^{*}L^{2}(X/Y)
and
L^{2}(f^{*}X/W)
have the same
transition functions with respect to
\{V_{ij}\}
.
∎

Consequently,

[D_{f^{*}X/W}^{\mathrm{sign}}]=[H_{f^{*}X/W}:=C_{0}(W,f^{*}L^{2}(X/Y;E)),\tilde{\phi}:C_{0}(f^{*}X)\to\mathbb{B}(H_{f^{*}X/W}),D_{f^{*}X/W}^{\mathrm{sign}}]

Notice that the left hand side of base change,
[D_{X/Y}^{\mathrm{sign}}]\otimes[f^{*}]
, is also equal to

(f^{*})_{*}[H_{X/Y}:=C_{0}(Y,L^{2}(X/Y;E)),\phi:C_{0}(X)\to\mathbb{B}(H_{X/Y}),D_{X/Y}^{\mathrm{sign}}]\\
\in\mathrm{KK}(C_{0}(X),C_{0}(W))

The definition we have given thus gives for

[D_{X/Y}^{\mathrm{sign}}]\otimes[f^{*}]
the following element

[H_{X/Y}\otimes_{f^{*}}C_{0}(W),\phi\otimes_{f^{*}}{\rm Id}:C_{0}(X)\to\mathbb{B}(H_{X/Y}\otimes_{f^{*}}C_{0}(W)),D_{X/Y}^{\mathrm{sign}}\otimes{\rm Id}]

The right hand side,
on the other hand, is equal to

(g^{*})^{*}[H_{f^{*}X/W}:=C_{0}(W,L^{2}(f^{*}X/W,g^{*}E)),\tilde{\phi}:C_{0}(f^{*}X)\to\mathbb{B}(H_{f^{*}X/W}),D_{f^{*}X/W}^{\mathrm{sign}}]\\
\in\mathrm{KK}(C_{0}(X),C_{0}(W))

which is in turn equal to

[H_{f^{*}X/W}:=C_{0}(W,L^{2}(f^{*}X/W,g^{*}E)),\tilde{\phi}\circ(g^{*}):C_{0}(X)\to\mathbb{B}(H_{f^{*}X/W}),D_{f^{*}X/W}^{\mathrm{sign}}]

that we can write, thanks to the lemma, as

[C_{0}(W,f^{*}L^{2}(X/Y,E)),\tilde{\phi}\circ(g^{*}):C_{0}(X)\to\mathbb{B}(C_{0}(W,f^{*}L^{2}(X/Y,E))),D_{f^{*}X/W}^{\mathrm{sign}}]

and this can be written as

[C_{0}(W,f^{*}L^{2}(X/Y,E)),\tilde{\phi}\circ(g^{*}):C_{0}(X)\to\mathbb{B}(C_{0}(W,f^{*}L^{2}(X/Y,E))),f^{*}D_{X/Y}^{\mathrm{sign}}]

because the vertical wedge metric on
f^{*}X
is the pull-back of the corresponding metric on
X.

This finishes the proof of Theorem
5.21
.
∎

Remark 5.23
.

It is convenient to note, as this will show up when we use the geometric description of K-homology below, that
Theorem
5.21
holds also when
f
is a continuous map from a smooth manifold into a smoothly stratified space.
In this case we define
[D_{f^{*}X/W}^{\mathrm{sign}}]
by

[C_{0}(W,f^{*}L^{2}(X/Y,E)),\tilde{\phi}:C_{0}(f^{*}X)\to\mathbb{B}(C_{0}(W,f^{*}L^{2}(X/Y,E))),f^{*}D_{X/Y}^{\mathrm{sign}}]

(so that only the continuity of
f
is required)
and the proof above shows that for any
\alpha\in\mathrm{KK}(C_{0}(W),\mathbb{C}),

[D_{X/Y}^{\mathrm{sign}}]\otimes(f_{*}(\alpha))=g_{*}([D_{f^{*}X/W}^{\mathrm{sign}}]\otimes\alpha).

An immediate corollary, obtained by passing to analytic transfer classes, is base change.

Corollary 5.24
.

If
p:X\longrightarrow Y
is a smooth oriented fiber bundle of stratified spaces, whose typical fiber
Z
is a Witt pseudomanifold,
W
is another smoothly stratified space and
f:W\longrightarrow Y
is a proper smooth stratified map and
q:f^{*}X\longrightarrow W
is the induced fiber bundle over
W,
then

\Sigma(p)\otimes f_{*}\alpha=g_{*}(\Sigma(q)\otimes\alpha),\quad\forall\alpha\in\mathrm{K}_{*}^{\mathrm{an}}(W)[\tfrac{1}{2}].

Equivalently,

p^{!}(f_{*}(\alpha))=g_{*}(q^{!}(\alpha))\quad\forall\alpha\in\mathrm{K}_{*}^{\mathrm{an}}(W)[\tfrac{1}{2}].

6.
Analytic transfer of the signature orientation along a normally non-singular inclusion

Definition 6.1
.

An inclusion of smoothly stratified spaces
j:X\longrightarrow Y
is said to be
normally non-singular
(nns) if there exist

•

an open neighborhood

\cal U\subseteq Y
of
j(X)
,

•

an oriented vector bundle
\pi:N_{X}Y\to X,
and

•

a stratified diffeomorphism
\psi:N_{X}Y\to\cal U
such that

\textstyle{N_{X}Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\psi}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{\cal U}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{j}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{j(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

commutes, where the left hand vertical map is the zero section inclusion.

Here
\cal U
has the stratification inherited from
Y
and
N_{X}Y
has the stratification pulled-back
from
X
through
\pi
.

We shall denote by
\phi:\cal U\to N_{X}Y
the inverse of
\psi
.

Let us start by recalling the situation in the smooth setting.
If
j:M\longrightarrow M^{\prime}
is an embedding of smooth manifolds then, without any orientation assumptions, we will construct a ‘wrong-way map’ between the K-theory of their tangent bundles,

\mathrm{K}^{0}(TM)\longrightarrow\mathrm{K}^{0}(TM^{\prime}).

If
M
and
M^{\prime}
are K-oriented then this induces the Gysin map
j_{!}:\mathrm{K}^{0}(M)\longrightarrow\mathrm{K}^{0}(M^{\prime})
(in fact it suffices for
j
to be K-oriented).

To define the former, we start by noting that
any embedding of smooth manifolds is normally non-singular since the image
j(M)
has a tubular neighborhood
\cal U,

j(M)\subseteq\cal U\subseteq M^{\prime},

which is diffeomorphic to the total space of the normal bundle
N=N_{M}M^{\prime}
of
j(M)
in
M^{\prime}
through a diffeomorphism

\varphi:\cal U\to N
.
The normal bundle of
Tj(M)
in
TM^{\prime}
is
TN
and this equals
N\oplus N
lifted to
Tj(M).
Since
N\oplus N
can be identified with
N\otimes\mathbb{C}

we see that
TN\longrightarrow Tj(M)
is a complex vector bundle so there is a KK-class corresponding to the fiberwise Dolbeault operators

[\overline{\partial}_{TN/Tj(M)}]\in\mathrm{KK}(C_{0}(TN),C_{0}(Tj(M)))

and multiplication by this class implements the Thom isomorphism so there is also a class

[\overline{\partial}_{TN/Tj(M)}]^{-1}\in\mathrm{KK}(C_{0}(Tj(M)),C_{0}(TN))

We can identify
T\cal U
with
TN
through
d\varphi:T\cal U\to TN
. This induces
by pull-back an algebra isomorphism
\Phi:C_{0}(TN)\to C_{0}(T\cal U)
.
As
T\cal U
is an open subset of
TM^{\prime},
the natural extension by zero defines a homomorphism of
C^{*}
-algebras
C_{0}(T\cal U)\longrightarrow C_{0}(TM^{\prime})
.
Precomposing with
\Phi
we finally obtain a
C^{*}
-algebra homomorphism
j^{\prime}:C_{0}(TN)\longrightarrow C_{0}(TM^{\prime})
. This defines a KK-class,

[j^{\prime}]\in\mathrm{KK}(C_{0}(TN),C_{0}(TM^{\prime})).

Putting these together we obtain

\textstyle{\mathrm{K}^{0}(TM)=\mathrm{KK}(\mathbb{C},C_{0}(TM))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\otimes[\overline{\partial}_{TN/Tj(M)}]^{-1}\phantom{xxxx}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{KK}(\mathbb{C},C_{0}(TM^{\prime}))=\mathrm{K}^{0}(TM^{\prime})}
\textstyle{\mathrm{KK}(\mathbb{C},C_{0}(TN))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\otimes[j^{\prime}]}

Remark 6.2
.

This construction produces the topological index of Atiyah-Singer in the case where
M^{\prime}=\mathbb{R}^{n}.

In that case
\mathrm{K}^{0}(TM^{\prime})=\mathrm{K}^{0}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})=\mathrm{K}^{0}(\mathbb{C}^{n})=\mathbb{Z}
and so we have produced a map

\mathrm{K}^{0}(TM)\longrightarrow\mathbb{Z}.

If
M
is K-oriented, i.e., if there is a spin-c structure on its tangent bundle, then the fiberwise spin-c Dirac operators
\eth_{TM/M}
determine a KK-class

[\eth_{TM/M}]\in\mathrm{KK}(C_{0}(TM),C(M))

and multiplication by this class implements the Thom isomorphism

\mathrm{K}^{0}(TM)=\mathrm{KK}(\mathbb{C},C_{0}(TM))\longrightarrow\mathrm{KK}(\mathbb{C},C(M))=\mathrm{K}^{0}(M).

Thus if both
M
and
M^{\prime}
are K-oriented we obtain the Gysin map

j_{!}:\mathrm{K}^{0}(M)\longrightarrow\mathrm{K}^{0}(M^{\prime})

as anticipated.

For our purposes it is useful to note that the Gysin map can be defined without reference to the tangent bundles assuming only that the inclusion
j:M\longrightarrow M^{\prime}
is K-oriented or, equivalently, that the normal bundle
N_{M}M^{\prime}
has a spin-c structure. In this case there is a
Thom isomorphism

\mathrm{K}^{0}(N_{M}M^{\prime})=\mathrm{KK}(\mathbb{C},C_{0}(N_{M}M^{\prime}))\longrightarrow\mathrm{KK}(\mathbb{C},C_{0}(M))=\mathrm{K}^{0}(M)

resulting from the Kasparov product with the class of the fiberwise spin-c Dirac operators (see
[
Kas80
, §5, Theorem 8]

[
Bla86
, §19.9.4]
).
Multiplying the inverse of this isomorphism with the class induced by the
C^{*}
-homomorphism
i:C_{0}(N_{M}M^{\prime})\longrightarrow C_{0}(M^{\prime})
(obtained by identifying
N_{M}M^{\prime}
with
\cal U
as above) produces the Gysin map

j_{!}:\mathrm{K}^{0}(M)\longrightarrow\mathrm{K}^{0}(M^{\prime})

and exhibits it as Kasparov product with

j!:=[\eth_{N_{M}M^{\prime}/M}]^{-1}\otimes[i]\in\mathrm{KK}(C_{0}(M),C_{0}(M^{\prime})).

We will refer to it as the
spin-c Gysin map
, to emphasize its origin and the assumption that

j
is
K
-oriented (that is, the normal bundle has a spin-c structure). We shall give a similar
construction in K-homology using the signature operator and we shall see that such a construction also holds in the context of normally non-singular inclusions of
Witt spaces.

Let
j:X\longrightarrow Y
be a nns inclusion of smoothly stratified spaces and recall that, by definition,
N_{Y}X
is oriented.
We have denoted the vector bundle projection by
\pi:N_{Y}X\longrightarrow X
. Then as a special case of the theory developed in the previous sections, the family of signature operators on the fibers of
N_{X}Y
determines an analytic transfer class,

\Sigma(\pi)=2^{-\lfloor\ell/2\rfloor}[D_{N_{Y}X/X}^{\mathrm{sign}}]\in\mathrm{KK}^{\ell}(C_{0}(N_{Y}X),C_{0}(X))[\tfrac{1}{2}]\,,\;\;\;\ell=\dim Y-\dim X.

Lemma 6.3
.

(Hilsum, see
[
Hil89
, Theoreme 3.10]
)

There exist a unique element
\Sigma_{N_{Y}X}\in\mathrm{KK}^{\ell}(C_{0}(X),C_{0}(N_{Y}X))[\tfrac{1}{2}]
such that

\Sigma_{N_{Y}X}\otimes\Sigma(\pi)=1\in{\operatorname{KK}}^{0}(C_{0}(X),C_{0}(X))[\tfrac{1}{2}],

Proof.

(Throughout the proof we abbreviate
\Sigma_{N_{Y}X}
as
\Sigma_{N}.
)
We follow closely Hilsum but use our Theorem
5.16
at a crucial point.
Consider a vector bundle
\nu\to X
such that
N_{X}Y\oplus\nu

is the trivial bundle
X\times\mathbb{R}^{m}
(up to bundle isomorphisms), with
m
even. We obtain a fibration

p:X\times\mathbb{R}^{m}\to N_{X}Y
and thus a class
[D_{(X\times\mathbb{R}^{m})/N_{X}Y}^{\mathrm{sign}}]\in\mathrm{KK}^{m-\ell}(C_{0}(X\times\mathbb{R}^{m}),C_{0}(N_{X}Y))
.
Recall that Kasparov in
[
Kas80
, Thereom 7.5]
constructed elements
\alpha_{m}\in\mathrm{KK}^{m}(C_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{m}),\mathbb{C})
and
\beta_{m}\in\mathrm{KK}^{m}(\mathbb{C},C_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{m}))

such that
\beta_{m}\otimes\alpha_{m}={\rm Id}_{\mathbb{C}}\in\mathrm{KK}^{0}(\mathbb{C},\mathbb{C})
. Moreover, as pointed out by Hilsum,

[D_{\mathbb{R}^{m}}^{\mathrm{sign}}]=2^{m}\alpha_{m}
.
Consider
\beta_{m}\in\mathrm{KK}^{m}(\mathbb{C},C_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{m}))
and denote by

\tau_{X}(\beta_{m})

the element in the group
\mathrm{KK}^{m}(C_{0}(X),C_{0}(X)\otimes C_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{m}))\equiv\mathrm{KK}^{m}(C_{0}(X),C_{0}(X\times\mathbb{R}^{m}))
obtained by external Kasparov product
of
\beta_{m}
with the KK element corresponding
to the identity homomorphism on
C_{0}(X)
:

\tau_{X}(\beta_{m}):=\beta_{m}\otimes{\rm Id}_{C_{0}(X)}\in\mathrm{KK}^{m}(C_{0}(X),C_{0}(X)\otimes C_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{m})).

Then

\tau_{X}(\beta_{m})\otimes[D_{(X\times\mathbb{R}^{m})/N_{X}Y}^{\mathrm{sign}}]\in\mathrm{KK}^{2m-\ell}(C_{0}(X),C_{0}(N_{X}Y))

Let us prove that

(\tau_{X}(\beta_{m})\otimes[D_{(X\times\mathbb{R}^{m})/N_{X}Y}^{\mathrm{sign}}])\otimes[D_{N_{Y}X/X}^{\mathrm{sign}}]=2^{m}\operatorname{Id}_{C_{0}(X)}\in\mathrm{KK}^{0}(C_{0}(X),C_{0}(X))\,.

We consider the projection of the trivial bundle
X\times\mathbb{R}^{m}\to X
and we denote it by
q
. Then, clearly,

q=\pi\circ p
and thus

(6.1)

[D_{(X\times\mathbb{R}^{m})/X}^{\mathrm{sign}}]=[D_{(X\times\mathbb{R}^{m})/N_{X}Y}^{\mathrm{sign}}]\otimes[D_{N_{Y}X/X}^{\mathrm{sign}}]

by our Theorem
5.16
.
But it is also true that
[D_{(X\times\mathbb{R}^{m})/X}^{\mathrm{sign}}]=\tau_{X}(\alpha_{m})
and so

\tau_{X}(\beta_{m})\otimes[D_{(X\times\mathbb{R}^{m})/X}^{\mathrm{sign}}]=2^{m}{\rm Id}_{C_{0}(X)}\,.

Thus multiplying (
6.1
) on the left by
\tau_{X}(\beta_{m})

and using associativity we obtain

2^{m}{\rm Id}_{C_{0}(X)}=(\tau_{X}(\beta_{m})\otimes[D_{(X\times\mathbb{R}^{m})/N_{X}Y}^{\mathrm{sign}}])\otimes[D_{N_{Y}X/X}^{\mathrm{sign}}]\;\;\text{in}\;\;\mathrm{KK}^{0}(C_{0}(X),C_{0}(X))\,.

which is what we wanted to establish.

If we rewrite this in terms of the analytic transfer forms, using the notation
j=\dim X,

k=\dim Y
(so
\ell=k-j
),
it becomes

2^{m}{\rm Id}_{C_{0}(X)}=(\tau_{X}(\beta_{m})\otimes 2^{\lfloor(m-k)/2\rfloor}\Sigma(\pi))\otimes 2^{\lfloor(k-j)/2\rfloor}\Sigma(p)\;\;\text{in}\;\;\mathrm{KK}^{0}(C_{0}(X),C_{0}(X))[\tfrac{1}{2}]\,.

We set

\Sigma_{N}:=2^{-m+\lfloor(m-k)/2\rfloor+\lfloor(k-j)/2\rfloor}\tau_{X}(\beta_{m})\otimes\Sigma(\pi)

and we notice that this is indeed an element in
\mathrm{KK}^{\ell}(C_{0}(X),C_{0}(N_{Y}X))[\tfrac{1}{2}]
and by construction it satisfies
the equation
\Sigma_{N}\otimes\Sigma(\pi)=1\in{\operatorname{KK}}^{0}(C_{0}(X),C_{0}(X))[\tfrac{1}{2}]
.

∎

We denote the element
\Sigma_{N_{Y}X}
as
\Sigma(\pi)^{-1}
.

The above result is in fact valid in greater generality. Let
V\xrightarrow{\pi}B
be an orientable
real vector bundle of rank
\ell
on a (possibly non-compact) Witt space
B
.
We know that there exists a well-defined element
[D_{V/B}^{\mathrm{sign}}]\in\mathrm{KK}^{\ell}(C_{0}(V),C_{0}(B))\,,\;\ell={\rm rank}\;V
, a corresponding analytic transfer class

\Sigma(\pi)=2^{-\lfloor\ell/2\rfloor}[D_{V/B}^{\mathrm{sign}}]\in\mathrm{KK}^{\ell}(C_{0}(V),C_{0}(B))[\tfrac{1}{2}]

and that this element induces a Gysin map by left Kasparov product

\pi^{!}:\mathrm{KK}^{j}(C_{0}(B),\mathbb{C})[\tfrac{1}{2}]\rightarrow\mathrm{KK}^{j+\ell}(C_{0}(V),\mathbb{C})[\tfrac{1}{2}].

Moreover
\pi^{!}\mathrm{sign}_{K}(B)=\mathrm{sign}_{K}(V)\in\mathrm{KK}^{\dim B+\ell}(C_{0}(V),\mathbb{C})[\tfrac{1}{2}]
.

Proceeding as above we understand that there exists
\Sigma_{V}\in\mathrm{KK}^{\ell}(C_{0}(B),C_{0}(V))[\tfrac{1}{2}]
such that
\Sigma_{V}\otimes\Sigma(\pi)=1\in{\operatorname{KK}}^{0}(C_{0}(B),C_{0}(B))[\tfrac{1}{2}]
.
We denote the element
\Sigma_{V}
by
\Sigma(\pi)^{-1}
.
Thus if
j:B\hookrightarrow V
is the zero embedding, then there exists a homomorphism

j^{!}:{\operatorname{KK}}^{i}(C_{0}(V),\mathbb{C})[\tfrac{1}{2}]\longrightarrow{\operatorname{KK}}^{i+\ell}(C_{0}(B),\mathbb{C})[\tfrac{1}{2}]

given by

j^{!}(-):=\Sigma(\pi)^{-1}\otimes-.

which is the inverse of
\pi^{!}
. Morover it is clear from the equality
\pi^{!}\mathrm{sign}_{K}(B)=\mathrm{sign}_{K}(V)

that
j^{!}\mathrm{sign}_{K}(V)=\mathrm{sign}_{K}(B)
in
{\operatorname{KK}}^{\dim B}(C_{0}(B),\mathbb{C})[\tfrac{1}{2}]
or, equivalently,

(6.2)

\Sigma(\pi)^{-1}\otimes\mathrm{sign}_{K}(V)=\mathrm{sign}_{K}(B)\;\;\text{in}\;\;{\operatorname{KK}}^{\dim B}(C_{0}(B),\mathbb{C})[\tfrac{1}{2}]\,.

We go back to our normally non-singular inclusion of Witt spaces
X\hookrightarrow Y
.
We have seen that there is then an orientable normal bundle

\pi:N_{Y}X\to X
, an open neighborhood
\mathcal{U}\subset Y
of
X
, and a
stratified diffeomorphism
\varphi:\mathcal{U}\longrightarrow X
.
We proceed in parallel to Brodzki, Mathai, Rosenberg, Szabo

[
BMRS09
, Example 3.3]
.
From the above discussion, see (
6.2
), we record that for the normal bundle
N_{Y}X\xrightarrow{\pi}X
it holds that

(6.3)

\Sigma(\pi)^{-1}\otimes\mathrm{sign}_{K}(N_{Y}X)=\mathrm{sign}_{K}(X);\;\text{in}\;\;{\operatorname{KK}}^{\dim X}(C_{0}(X),\mathbb{C})[\tfrac{1}{2}]\,.

The open inclusion
i:\mathcal{U}\subset Y
defines
an element

i!\in{\operatorname{KK}}^{m}(C_{0}(U),C_{0}(Y)),\;\;\;\forall m\in\mathbb{N},

corresponding to the homomorphism of
C_{0}(U)
in
C_{0}(Y)
obtained by extension by zero.
(This works for any open subset
U
of a locally compact space
Y
.)
This defines by Kasparov multiplication on the left a
restriction homomorphism

{\operatorname{KK}}^{\ell}(C_{0}(Y),\mathbb{C})\to{\operatorname{KK}}^{\ell}(C_{0}(\mathcal{U}),\mathbb{C})
,
\forall\ell\in\mathbb{N}
.
Note that
\mathcal{U}
is a Witt space and hence
there exists a signature class
[D_{\mathcal{U}}^{\mathrm{sign}}]\in{\operatorname{KK}}^{\dim Y}(C_{0}(\mathcal{U}),\mathbb{C})

and a corresponding analytic orientation class

\mathrm{sign}_{K}(\cal U)=2^{-\lfloor\dim Y/2\rfloor}[D_{\mathcal{U}}^{\mathrm{sign}}]\in{\operatorname{KK}}^{\dim Y}(C_{0}(\mathcal{U}),\mathbb{C})[\tfrac{1}{2}].

Lemma 6.4
.

Restriction to open subsets preserves
{\operatorname{KK}}
-classes of signature operators
of Witt spaces, i.e.

i!\otimes[D_{Y}^{\mathrm{sign}}]=[D_{\mathcal{U}}^{\mathrm{sign}}]\in{\operatorname{KK}}^{\dim Y}(C_{0}(\mathcal{U}),\mathbb{C}).

Hence we also have

i!\otimes\mathrm{sign}_{K}(Y)=\mathrm{sign}_{K}(\cal U)\in{\operatorname{KK}}^{\dim Y}(C_{0}(\mathcal{U}),\mathbb{C})[\tfrac{1}{2}].

Proof.

This is discussed in
[
Hil89
, Rem. 2.17, p. 419]
(take
N=point
there).
∎

Next,
the stratified diffeomorphism

\varphi:\mathcal{U}\longrightarrow N_{Y}X

gives an invertible element

[\varphi]\in{\operatorname{KK}}^{0}(C_{0}(N_{Y}X),C_{0}(\mathcal{U}))

which induces an isomorphism
{\operatorname{KK}}^{j}(C_{0}(\mathcal{U}),\mathbb{C})\to{\operatorname{KK}}^{j}(C_{0}(N_{Y}X),\mathbb{C})

by left Kasparov product.
By Proposition

4.1
(stratified diffeomorphism invariance
of the signature class on a Witt space)
we have

Lemma 6.5
.

It holds that

[\varphi]\otimes[D_{\mathcal{U}}^{\mathrm{sign}}]=[D_{N_{Y}X}^{\mathrm{sign}}].

We are in the position to define the Gysin restriction homomorphism

j^{!}
associated to the normally non-singular inclusion

j:X\hookrightarrow Y
of our Witt spaces.

Definition 6.6
.

Let
j:X\hookrightarrow Y
be a normally non-singular inclusion of Witt spaces.
Let
\ell
be the codimension of the inclusion. Then
Kasparov multiplication

{\operatorname{KK}}^{\ell}(C_{0}(X),C_{0}(N_{Y}X))[\mbox{$\frac{1}{2}$}]\otimes{\operatorname{KK}}^{0}(C_{0}(N_{Y}X),C_{0}(\mathcal{U}))[\mbox{$\frac{1}{2}$}]\otimes{\operatorname{KK}}^{0}(C_{0}(\mathcal{U}),C_{0}(Y))[\mbox{$\frac{1}{2}$}]

\longrightarrow{\operatorname{KK}}^{\ell}(C_{0}(X),C_{0}(Y))[\mbox{$\frac{1}{2}$}]

defines an element

(6.4)

\Sigma(j):=\Sigma(\pi)^{-1}\otimes[\varphi]\otimes i!\in{\operatorname{KK}}^{\ell}(C_{0}(X),C_{0}(Y))[\tfrac{1}{2}],

which we refer to as the
analytic transfer map of the nns inclusion
j
.
Kasparov multiplication with this element on the left defines the analytic Gysin restriction

j^{!}:{\operatorname{KK}}^{m+\ell}(C_{0}(Y),\mathbb{C})[\tfrac{1}{2}]\longrightarrow{\operatorname{KK}}^{m}(C(X),\mathbb{C})[\tfrac{1}{2}],

j^{!}(-):=\Sigma(j)\otimes-.

We have, finally, the following result:

Theorem 6.7
.

Let
X,Y
be Witt spaces
and let
j:X\hookrightarrow Y
be a normally non-singular inclusion of codimension
\ell
.
Then Gysin restriction
on analytic K-homology

j^{!}:{\operatorname{KK}}^{*+\ell}(C_{0}(Y),\mathbb{C})[\tfrac{1}{2}]\longrightarrow{\operatorname{KK}}^{*}(C_{0}(X),\mathbb{C})[\tfrac{1}{2}],

sends the analytic signature orientation class of
Y
to the
analytic signature orientation class of
X
, i.e.

(6.5)

j^{!}\mathrm{sign}_{K}(Y)=\mathrm{sign}_{K}(X).

Proof.

Using
6.3

and Lemmas
6.4
,
6.5
, we have

\displaystyle j^{!}\mathrm{sign}_{K}(Y)

\displaystyle=\Sigma(\pi)^{-1}\otimes[\varphi]\otimes i!\otimes\mathrm{sign}_{K}(Y)

\displaystyle=\Sigma(\pi)^{-1}\otimes[\varphi]\otimes\mathrm{sign}_{K}(\cal U)

\displaystyle=\Sigma(\pi)^{-1}\otimes\mathrm{sign}_{K}(N_{Y}X)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{sign}_{K}(X).

∎

Remark 6.8
.

Notice that even assuming the normal bundle
N_{Y}X
to be spin-c, we would not be able to use the
spin-c Gysin map in order to
obtain the analogue of (
6.5
) for the spin-c Dirac operator; indeed, we do not know, in general, that the spin-c Dirac operator defines a K-homology class in the stratified setting.

Functoriality in this case is simple to establish (see
[
Hil89
, §4.1]
).

Theorem 6.9
.

If
X,

Y,
and
Z
are Witt spaces and

X\xhookrightarrow{\phantom{x}i\phantom{x}}Y\xhookrightarrow{\phantom{x}j\phantom{x}}Z

are nns inclusions, then

\Sigma(j\circ i)=\Sigma(i)\otimes\Sigma(j)\in\mathrm{KK}(C_{0}(X),C_{0}(Z))[\tfrac{1}{2}]

Similarly, we can deduce base change from work we have already carried out.

Proposition 6.10
.

(Base Change for Gysin Restriction to Zero-Sections)
Let
Y
be a smoothly stratified compact Witt space and
p:X\to Y
an
oriented real vector bundle of rank
r
over
Y
.
Suppose that
W
is a compact smoothly stratified space and
f:W\to Y
a smooth stratified
map. Consider the Cartesian diagram

\textstyle{X_{W}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p_{W}}
\scriptstyle{g}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p}
\textstyle{W\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{Y.}

Then the base change formula

f_{*}\circ j^{!}_{W}=j^{!}\circ g_{*}

holds for the Gysin restrictions

j^{!}:{\operatorname{KK}}^{n}(C_{0}(X),\mathbb{C})[\tfrac{1}{2}]\to{\operatorname{KK}}^{n-r}(C(Y),\mathbb{C})[\tfrac{1}{2}],\quad j^{!}_{W}:{\operatorname{KK}}^{n}(C_{0}(X_{W}),\mathbb{C})[\tfrac{1}{2}]\to{\operatorname{KK}}^{n-r}(C(W),\mathbb{C})[\tfrac{1}{2}]

associated to the zero-section inclusions

j:Y\hookrightarrow X
and
j_{W}:W\hookrightarrow X_{W}
.

Proof.

According to Theorem
5.21
,

p^{!}f_{*}=g_{*}p^{!}_{W}
.
Recall that for vector bundles,
j^{!}
and
p^{!}
are mutually inverse
(Thom-) isomorphisms.
An application of
j^{!}
from the left on both sides yields

f_{*}=j^{!}p^{!}f_{*}=j^{!}g_{*}p^{!}_{W}.

Applying
j^{!}_{W}
from the right on both sides, we obtain

f_{*}j^{!}_{W}=j^{!}g_{*}p^{!}_{W}j^{!}_{W}=j^{!}g_{*}.

∎

7.
Compatibility of Analytic and Topological Orientations

7.1.
Prelude: The Four-Sphere

We illustrate the orientation classes considered in this work
for the case of a
4
-sphere, where these classes are determined
by their Chern characters.
Let
\nu
be the trivial real 4-plane bundle over a point.
Thus the total space of
\nu
is
4
and its Thom space
is
\operatorname{Th}(\nu)=S^{4}
, the
4
-sphere.
We shall describe the (real) Sullivan orientation

\Delta(\nu)\in{\widetilde{\operatorname{KO}}}^{4}(\operatorname{Th}(\nu))[\mbox{$\frac{1}{2}$}]={\widetilde{\operatorname{KO}}}^{4}(S^{4})[\mbox{$\frac{1}{2}$}]

of the bundle
\nu
, the Sullivan orientation

\Delta(S^{4})\in{\operatorname{KO}}_{4}(S^{4})[\mbox{$\frac{1}{2}$}]

of the manifold
S^{4}
, and its relation to the
{\operatorname{K}}
-homology class
of the signature operator on
S^{4}
.
Let

\beta=[H]-[1]\in{\widetilde{\operatorname{K}}}^{0}(S^{2})={\widetilde{\operatorname{K}}}^{-2}(S^{0})=\pi_{2}({\operatorname{K}})

be the Bott generator. The complex line bundle
H
on
S^{2}=\mathbb{C}P^{1}

is holomorphic and dual to the Hopf line bundle whose fiber over
a point in
\mathbb{C}P^{1}
is the complex line in
\mathbb{C}^{2}
represented by
the point.
Let
u:=c_{1}(H)\in H^{2}(S^{2};\mathbb{Z})
be the canonical generator
given by the first Chern class of
H
.
The Chern character is an isomorphism

\operatorname{ch}:{\widetilde{\operatorname{K}}}^{0}(S^{2})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\longrightarrow}}H^{2}(S^{2};\mathbb{Z})\cong\mathbb{Z}

given by
\operatorname{ch}(\beta)=u
.
We shall use the external products

\wedge:\widetilde{H}^{*}(X)\otimes\widetilde{H}^{*}(Y)\longrightarrow\widetilde{H}^{*}(X\wedge Y),\quad\wedge:\widetilde{{\operatorname{K}}}(X)\otimes\widetilde{{\operatorname{K}}}(Y)\longrightarrow\widetilde{{\operatorname{K}}}(X\wedge Y).

Note that for

X=S^{2},

Y=S^{2},
we have
S^{2}\wedge S^{2}=S^{4}.

The map

\widetilde{H}^{*}(S^{2};\mathbb{Z})\xrightarrow{\phantom{x}-\wedge u\phantom{x}}\widetilde{H}^{*}(S^{4};\mathbb{Z})

is an isomorphism, and by Bott periodicity, the Bott map

{\operatorname{Per}}_{\mathbb{C}}:\widetilde{{\operatorname{K}}}(S^{2})\xrightarrow{\phantom{x}-\wedge\beta\phantom{x}}\widetilde{{\operatorname{K}}}(S^{4})

is an isomorphism as well.
As the diagram

\textstyle{\widetilde{{\operatorname{K}}}(S^{2})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{ch}}
\scriptstyle{-\wedge\beta}
\textstyle{\widetilde{{\operatorname{K}}}(S^{4})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{ch}}
\textstyle{\widetilde{H}^{*}(S^{2};\mathbb{Z})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{-\wedge u}
\textstyle{\widetilde{H}^{*}(S^{4};\mathbb{Z})}

commutes (Husemoller
[
Hus94
, p. 308]
), we have

\operatorname{ch}(\beta^{2})=\operatorname{ch}(\beta\wedge\beta)=u\wedge u=u^{2}.

Let
c:{\operatorname{KO}}\to{\operatorname{K}}
be complexification as a morphism of ring spectra,
inducing homomorphisms

c_{*}:{\operatorname{KO}}^{*}(X)\to{\operatorname{K}}^{*}(X),\quad c_{*}:{\operatorname{KO}}_{*}(X)\to{\operatorname{K}}_{*}(X).

The Pontrjagin character
\operatorname{ph}=\operatorname{ch}\circ c_{*}
localizes to

\operatorname{ph}[\mbox{$\frac{1}{2}$}]
given by the composition

\textstyle{\widetilde{{\operatorname{KO}}}^{0}(S^{4})[\mbox{$\frac{1}{2}$}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{ph}[\mbox{$\frac{1}{2}$}]}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\scriptstyle{c_{*}[\mbox{$\frac{1}{2}$}]}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{\widetilde{{\operatorname{K}}}^{0}(S^{4})[\mbox{$\frac{1}{2}$}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{ch}[\mbox{$\frac{1}{2}$}]}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{H^{4}(S^{4};\mathbb{Z})[\mbox{$\frac{1}{2}$}].}

Here we have used suspension isomorphisms to identify

\textstyle{\widetilde{{\operatorname{KO}}}^{0}(S^{4})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{c_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\textstyle{\widetilde{{\operatorname{KO}}}^{-4}(S^{0})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\operatorname{KO}}^{-4}(\operatorname{pt})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\pi_{4}({\operatorname{KO}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{c_{*}}
\textstyle{\widetilde{{\operatorname{K}}}^{0}(S^{4})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\textstyle{\widetilde{{\operatorname{K}}}^{-4}(S^{0})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\operatorname{K}}^{-4}(\operatorname{pt})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\pi_{4}({\operatorname{K}}).}

(Note that
c_{*}
commutes with suspension isomorphisms, since it is
induced by a morphism of spectra.)
On
\pi_{4}
, complexification induces multiplication by two,

c_{*}=2:\pi_{4}({\operatorname{KO}})=\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{Z}=\pi_{4}(K)
.
Let

a\in\pi_{4}({\operatorname{KO}})[\mbox{$\frac{1}{2}$}]={\widetilde{\operatorname{KO}}}^{-4}(S^{0})[\mbox{$\frac{1}{2}$}]={\widetilde{\operatorname{KO}}}^{0}(S^{4})[\mbox{$\frac{1}{2}$}]=\mathbb{Z}[\mbox{$\frac{1}{2}$}]

be the unique element with

c_{*}(a)=\beta^{2}.

This is the generator used in

[
Ban25
]
and
by Oscar Randal-Williams in
[
RW24
]
.
Its Pontrjagin character satisfies

\operatorname{ph}[\mbox{$\frac{1}{2}$}](a)=\operatorname{ch}[\mbox{$\frac{1}{2}$}](\beta^{2})=u^{2}.

The generator
u^{2}\in H^{4}(S^{4};\mathbb{Z})
agrees with
the Thom class
u(\nu)\in H^{4}(S^{4};\mathbb{Z})
of the trivial rank

4
-bundle
\nu
over a point.
By construction of the Sullivan orientation
(see also
[
Ban25
, p. 13, Lemma 3.8]
),

\operatorname{ph}[\mbox{$\frac{1}{2}$}](\Delta(\nu))=L^{-1}(\nu)\cup u(\nu)=u(\nu)\\
=u^{2}=\operatorname{ph}[\mbox{$\frac{1}{2}$}](a).

Since
\operatorname{ph}[\mbox{$\frac{1}{2}$}]
is an isomorphism,

\Delta(\nu)=a.

This is Lemma 3.9 in
[
Ban25
]
for the case of a
4
-plane bundle.
Since
\Delta(S^{4})\in{\operatorname{KO}}_{4}(S^{4})[\mbox{$\frac{1}{2}$}]
is an orientation,
we have

(7.1)

\Delta(S^{4})=\sigma_{*}(1),

\sigma_{*}:{\widetilde{\operatorname{KO}}}_{0}(S^{0})[\mbox{$\frac{1}{2}$}]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\widetilde{\operatorname{KO}}}_{4}(S^{4})[\mbox{$\frac{1}{2}$}]

is the suspension isomorphism and

1\in\pi_{0}({\operatorname{KO}}[\mbox{$\frac{1}{2}$}])={\widetilde{\operatorname{KO}}}_{0}(S^{0})[\mbox{$\frac{1}{2}$}]
the unit of the ring

\pi_{*}({\operatorname{KO}}[\mbox{$\frac{1}{2}$}])
.
Another way to see this is as follows:
The map
\Delta_{\operatorname{SO}}:\operatorname{MSO}\to{\operatorname{KO}}[\mbox{$\frac{1}{2}$}]
has been constructed
in
[
Ban25
]
as a morphism of ring spectra. In particular, the diagram

\textstyle{\widetilde{\operatorname{MSO}}_{4}(S^{4})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Delta_{*}}
\textstyle{{\widetilde{\operatorname{KO}}}_{4}(S^{4})[\mbox{$\frac{1}{2}$}]}
\textstyle{\widetilde{\operatorname{MSO}}_{0}(S^{0})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sigma_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\Delta_{*}}
\textstyle{{\widetilde{\operatorname{KO}}}_{0}(S^{0})[\mbox{$\frac{1}{2}$}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sigma_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}

commutes and
\Delta_{*}(1)=1\in{\widetilde{\operatorname{KO}}}_{0}(S^{0})[\mbox{$\frac{1}{2}$}]
.
The reduced bordism groups
\widetilde{\operatorname{MSO}}_{n}(X)
are given by the kernel of

\operatorname{MSO}_{n}(X)\to\operatorname{MSO}_{n}(\operatorname{pt})),

so that
[f:M^{n}\to X]\in\operatorname{MSO}_{n}(X)
is an element of

\widetilde{\operatorname{MSO}}_{n}(X)
if and only if
[M]=0\in\operatorname{MSO}_{n}
.
Since
[S^{4}]=0\in\operatorname{MSO}_{4}
,
it follows that
[\operatorname{id}_{S^{4}}]\in\widetilde{\operatorname{MSO}}_{4}(S^{4})
.
The
\operatorname{MSO}
-orientation of a smooth oriented closed manifold
M^{n}

is given by the identity map
[\operatorname{id}_{M}]\in\operatorname{MSO}_{n}(M)
and

[\operatorname{id}_{S^{4}}]=\sigma_{*}(1)\in\widetilde{\operatorname{MSO}}_{4}(S^{4}).

By definition,
\Delta(S^{4})=\Delta_{*}[\operatorname{id}_{S^{4}}].

It follows that indeed

\Delta(S^{4})=\Delta_{*}\sigma_{*}(1)=\sigma_{*}\Delta_{*}(1)=\sigma_{*}(1).

Let
\Psi^{2}:{\operatorname{KO}}[\mbox{$\frac{1}{2}$}]\to{\operatorname{KO}}[\mbox{$\frac{1}{2}$}]
be the stable Adams operation,
constructed as a morphism of
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-ring spectra.
Thus the diagram

\textstyle{{\widetilde{\operatorname{KO}}}_{4}(S^{4})[\mbox{$\frac{1}{2}$}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Psi^{2}}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{{\widetilde{\operatorname{KO}}}_{4}(S^{4})[\mbox{$\frac{1}{2}$}]}
\textstyle{{\widetilde{\operatorname{KO}}}_{0}(S^{0})[\mbox{$\frac{1}{2}$}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sigma_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\Psi^{2}}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{{\widetilde{\operatorname{KO}}}_{0}(S^{0})[\mbox{$\frac{1}{2}$}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sigma_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}

commutes and
\Psi^{2}(1)=1\in{\widetilde{\operatorname{KO}}}_{0}(S^{0})[\mbox{$\frac{1}{2}$}]
.
Using (
7.1
), we deduce that

(7.2)

\Psi^{2}\Delta(S^{4})=\Psi^{2}\sigma_{*}(1)=\sigma_{*}\Psi^{2}(1)=\sigma_{*}(1)=\Delta(S^{4}).

Remark 7.1
.

The
4
-fold periodicity of
{\operatorname{KO}}[\mbox{$\frac{1}{2}$}]
does
not
commute
with the stable Adams operation
\Psi^{2}
.
Indeed, Sullivan’s periodicity map

{\operatorname{Per}}:{\operatorname{KO}}^{0}(X)[\mbox{$\frac{1}{2}$}]\xrightarrow{\phantom{x}-\wedge a\phantom{x}}{\operatorname{KO}}^{0}(X\wedge S^{4})[\mbox{$\frac{1}{2}$}]

is given by multiplication with
a=\Delta(\nu)
,
see Sullivan
[
Sul71
, p. 202]
.
The stable Adams operation

\Psi^{2}:\pi_{4k}({\operatorname{KO}}[\mbox{$\frac{1}{2}$}])\to\pi_{4k}({\operatorname{KO}}[\mbox{$\frac{1}{2}$}])

acts on the preferred generator by

\Psi^{2}(a^{k})=4^{k}\cdot a^{k}.

Hence

\Psi^{2}{\operatorname{Per}}(1)=\Psi^{2}(a)=4a,

which does not equal

{\operatorname{Per}}\Psi^{2}(1)={\operatorname{Per}}(1)=a.

The
4
-sphere is not an almost complex manifold, but since it
is oriented and
w_{2}(S^{4})=0
, it is a spin manifold.
The spin structures on
S^{4}
are in one-to-one correspondence
with elements of
H^{1}(S^{4};\mathbb{Z}/_{2})=0
. Thus
S^{4}
has a unique
spin structure, which determines in particular a canonical
{\operatorname{Spin}}^{c}
-structure
on
S^{4}
.
Therefore,
S^{4}
has an integral Atiyah-Bott-Shapiro orientation

[S^{4}]_{\operatorname{ABS}}\in{\operatorname{K}}_{4}(S^{4}).

Since
c_{*}
, being induced by a morphism
c
of ring spectra,
is a natural transformation of homology theories, the diagram

\textstyle{{\widetilde{\operatorname{KO}}}_{4}(S^{4})[\mbox{$\frac{1}{2}$}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{c_{*}}
\textstyle{{\widetilde{\operatorname{K}}}_{4}(S^{4})[\mbox{$\frac{1}{2}$}]}
\textstyle{{\widetilde{\operatorname{KO}}}_{0}(S^{0})[\mbox{$\frac{1}{2}$}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sigma_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\scriptstyle{c_{*}}
\textstyle{{\widetilde{\operatorname{K}}}_{0}(S^{0})[\mbox{$\frac{1}{2}$}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sigma_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}

commutes and
c_{*}(1)=1\in{\widetilde{\operatorname{K}}}_{0}(S^{0})[\mbox{$\frac{1}{2}$}]
.
Since
[S^{4}]_{\operatorname{ABS}}
is an orientation, the equation

[S^{4}]_{\operatorname{ABS}}=\sigma_{*}(1)

holds; see Atiyah-Bott-Shapiro
[
ABS64
, p. 30]
for the cohomological case.
Using (
7.2
), we compute

c_{*}(\Psi^{2})^{-1}\Delta(S^{4})=c_{*}\Delta(S^{4})=c_{*}\sigma_{*}(1)=\sigma_{*}c_{*}(1)=\sigma_{*}(1),

which implies

(7.3)

c_{*}(\Psi^{2})^{-1}\Delta(S^{4})=[S^{4}]_{\operatorname{ABS}}.

Now let
D^{\operatorname{sign}}=d+d^{*}
denote the signature operator with respect to some Riemannian
metric on
S^{4}
and let

[D^{\operatorname{sign}}]\in{\operatorname{K}}^{\operatorname{an}}_{4}(S^{4})

be the associated class in analytic
{\operatorname{K}}
-homology.
(This class exists integrally, not just
away from
2
.)
The Atiyah-Singer
\mathcal{L}
-classes were introduced in

[
AS68
, p. 577]
.
The unstable cohomological Atiyah-Singer class
\mathcal{L}^{*}_{u}
is associated to the
power series

x\coth(x/2)=2+\frac{1}{6}x^{2}-\frac{1}{360}x^{4}+\frac{1}{15120}x^{6}-\frac{1}{604800}x^{8}\pm\ldots.

(The stable Atiyah-Singer class
\mathcal{L}^{*}_{s}
is
associated to
(x/2)\coth(x/2)
, but will not be used here.
The Hirzebruch
L
-class is associated to
x\coth x
.
In
[
Sto68
, p. 200]
, Stong denotes
\mathcal{L}^{*}_{u}
by
\delta
.)
For a
4
-dimensional manifold
M^{4},

\mathcal{L}^{*}_{u}(M^{4})=4+\frac{1}{3}p_{1}(TM).

Since the
4
-sphere is stably parallelizable,

\mathcal{L}^{*}_{u}(S^{4})=4.

The homological unstable Atiyah-Singer class
\mathcal{L}^{u}_{*}
is the Poincaré
dual
\mathcal{L}^{u}_{*}=\mathcal{L}^{*}_{u}\cap[M]_{H}
,
where
[M]_{H}\in H_{n}(M;\mathbb{Z})
denotes the fundamental class
in ordinary integral homology of a closed,
H\mathbb{Z}
-oriented
n
-dimensional
manifold
M
.
Thus

\mathcal{L}^{u}_{*}(S^{4})=4[S^{4}]_{H}.

In fact, for an even-dimensional smooth manifold,
the unstable Atiyah-Singer
\mathcal{L}
-class is given by

\mathcal{L}^{u}_{*}(M)=\sum_{j}2^{j}L_{2j}(M)\in H_{*}(M;\mathbb{Q}),

where the classes
L_{2j}(M)\in H_{2j}(M;\mathbb{Q})
are the components of the
Poincaré dual
L_{*}(M)=L^{*}(TM)\cap[M]_{H}
of the Hirzebruch
L
-class

L^{*}(TM)
, see also Moscovici-Wu
[
MW97
, p. 14]
.
Under the Chern character, the
{\operatorname{K}}
-class of the signature operator
is a refinement of the unstable Atiyah-Singer class.
This follows from the Atiyah-Singer index theorem, see also
Rosenberg-Weinberger
[
RW06
, p. 48]
.
Thus

\operatorname{ch}[D^{\operatorname{sign}}]=\mathcal{L}^{u}_{*}(S^{4})

and it follows that

\operatorname{ch}[D^{\operatorname{sign}}]=4[S^{4}]_{H}.

Note that since the signature of
S^{4}
vanishes,
[D^{\operatorname{sign}}]
is actually a
class in reduced
{\operatorname{K}}
-homology,
[D^{\operatorname{sign}}]\in{\widetilde{\operatorname{K}}}_{4}(S^{4})
.
Under the standard isomorphism

\varphi:{\operatorname{K}}^{\operatorname{geo}}_{*}(X)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\operatorname{K}}^{\operatorname{top}}_{*}(X),\quad\varphi[M,E,f]=f_{*}([E]\cap[M]_{\operatorname{ABS}}),

the topological homological Chern character
\operatorname{ch}:{\operatorname{K}}^{\operatorname{top}}_{*}(X)\to H_{*}(X;\mathbb{Q})

corresponds to the homological Chern character

\operatorname{ch}:{\operatorname{K}}^{\operatorname{geo}}_{*}(X)\to H_{*}(X;\mathbb{Q})
given by

\operatorname{ch}[M,E,f]=f_{*}(\operatorname{ch}(E)\cup{\operatorname{td}}(TM)\cap[M]_{H}),

see e.g. Jakob
[
Jak98
, p. 77, 4.2]
.
Here one uses the interesting fact that the Todd class
{\operatorname{td}}(-),

a priori only defined for complex bundles, actually survives to

{\operatorname{Spin}}^{c}
-vector bundles
T
, such as the tangent bundle

TM
. Indeed,
{\operatorname{Spin}}^{c}
vector bundles have a first Chern class
c_{1}(T)

(the integral class reducing to
w_{2}(T)
which is defined by the

{\operatorname{Spin}}^{c}
-structure) and one then defines

{\operatorname{td}}(T)=e^{c_{1}(T)/2}\hat{A}(T)

in rational cohomology (Baum-Douglas
[
BD82
, p. 136]
),
where
\hat{A}(T)
is the
\hat{A}
-polynomial in the Pontrjagin classes
of
T
.
For
S^{4}
,
c_{1}(TS^{4})=0\in H^{2}(S^{4})=0
and

\hat{A}(TS^{4})=1-\frac{1}{24}p_{1}(TS^{4})=1.

Thus
{\operatorname{td}}(TS^{4})=1
and the fundamental class

[S^{4},E=1,f=\operatorname{id}]\in K^{\operatorname{geo}}_{4}(S^{4})
has Chern character

\operatorname{ch}[S^{4},1,\operatorname{id}]=\operatorname{id}_{*}(\operatorname{ch}(1)\cup{\operatorname{td}}(TS^{4})\cap[S^{4}]_{H})=[S^{4}]_{H}.

Since
\operatorname{ch}\circ\varphi=\operatorname{ch}
, we get

\operatorname{ch}[S^{4}]_{\operatorname{ABS}}=\operatorname{ch}(\operatorname{id}_{*}(1\cap[S^{4}]_{\operatorname{ABS}}))=\operatorname{ch}\varphi[S^{4},1,\operatorname{id}]\\
=\operatorname{ch}[S^{4},1,\operatorname{id}],

and thus

\operatorname{ch}[S^{4}]_{\operatorname{ABS}}=[S^{4}]_{H}.
Using the standard isomorphism

\mu:{\operatorname{K}}^{\operatorname{geo}}_{*}(X)\to{\operatorname{K}}^{\operatorname{an}}_{*}(X)

(Baum-Douglas
[
BD82
]
),
we consider the diagram

\textstyle{K_{4}^{\operatorname{top}}(S^{4})\otimes\mathbb{Z}[\mbox{$\frac{1}{2}$}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{loc}}
\textstyle{K^{\operatorname{geo}}_{4}(S^{4})\otimes\mathbb{Z}[\mbox{$\frac{1}{2}$}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{loc}}
\scriptstyle{\mu}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{K^{\operatorname{an}}_{4}(S^{4})\otimes\mathbb{Z}[\mbox{$\frac{1}{2}$}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{loc}}
\textstyle{K_{4}^{\operatorname{top}}(S^{4})\otimes\mathbb{Q}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{ch}_{\mathbb{Q}}}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{K^{\operatorname{geo}}_{4}(S^{4})\otimes\mathbb{Q}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varphi_{\mathbb{Q}}}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{ch}_{\mathbb{Q}}}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\mu_{\mathbb{Q}}}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{K^{\operatorname{an}}_{4}(S^{4})\otimes\mathbb{Q}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{ch}_{\mathbb{Q}}}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{H_{2*}(S^{4};\mathbb{Q}).}

The vertical arrows are localization maps induced by tensoring with

\mathbb{Z}[\mbox{$\frac{1}{2}$}]\hookrightarrow\mathbb{Q}
.
These maps are injective, since there is no torsion in the

{\operatorname{K}}
-homology of
S^{4}
. The left-hand part of the diagram commutes
by Jakob
[
Jak98
, p. 77, 4.2]
and the right hand part commutes
according to Baum-Douglas
[
BD82
, p. 154]
.
For the Chern characters on the rational groups we have

\displaystyle\operatorname{ch}_{\mathbb{Q}}\operatorname{loc}(\mu\varphi^{-1})[S^{4}]_{\operatorname{ABS}}

\displaystyle=\operatorname{ch}_{\mathbb{Q}}\operatorname{loc}[S^{4}]_{\operatorname{ABS}}=\operatorname{ch}[S^{4}]_{\operatorname{ABS}}=[S^{4}]_{H}

\displaystyle=\operatorname{ch}(\mbox{$\frac{1}{4}$}[D^{\operatorname{sign}}])=\operatorname{ch}_{\mathbb{Q}}\operatorname{loc}(\mbox{$\frac{1}{4}$}[D^{\operatorname{sign}}]).

Since
\operatorname{ch}_{\mathbb{Q}}
is an isomorphism, it follows that

\operatorname{loc}(\mu\varphi^{-1})[S^{4}]_{\operatorname{ABS}}=\operatorname{loc}(\mbox{$\frac{1}{4}$}[D^{\operatorname{sign}}]).

As
\operatorname{loc}
is injective,

\mu\varphi^{-1}[S^{4}]_{\operatorname{ABS}}=\mbox{$\frac{1}{4}$}[D^{\operatorname{sign}}].

By (
7.3
),

\mu\varphi^{-1}c_{*}(\Psi^{2})^{-1}\Delta(S^{4})=\mu\varphi^{-1}[S^{4}]_{\operatorname{ABS}}=\mbox{$\frac{1}{4}$}[D^{\operatorname{sign}}],

a relation that we will first generalize to arbitrary oriented smooth
manifolds in Theorem
7.2
,
and then further to singular (Witt)
spaces in Theorem
7.6
.

7.2.
The Manifold Case.

For finite CW pairs
(X,A)
, we identify topological and analytic complex K-homology
via a natural isomorphism

(7.4)

{\operatorname{K}}^{\operatorname{top}}_{*}(X,A)\cong{\operatorname{K}}^{\operatorname{an}}_{*}(X,A)

of homology theories as in
[
LNS23
]
and
[
RW06
]
.
Let

\Psi^{2}:{\operatorname{KO}}[\mbox{$\frac{1}{2}$}]\longrightarrow{\operatorname{KO}}[\mbox{$\frac{1}{2}$}]

be the stable Adams operation, constructed as a morphism of

\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-ring spectra. This is an equivalence,
let
(\Psi^{2})^{-1}
denote an inverse.
Let

c:{\operatorname{KO}}\longrightarrow{\operatorname{K}}

denote complexification as a morphism of ring spectra.

Theorem 7.2
.

Let
M
be an
n
-dimensional closed oriented Riemannian manifold and

\Delta_{\operatorname{SO}}(M)\in{\operatorname{KO}}^{\operatorname{top}}_{n}(M)[\mbox{$\frac{1}{2}$}]

its Sullivan orientation,
[
Sul71
]
.
Under the above identification (
7.4
), the element

c(\Psi^{2})^{-1}\Delta_{\operatorname{SO}}(M)\in{\operatorname{K}}^{\operatorname{top}}_{n}(M)[\mbox{$\frac{1}{2}$}]

corresponds to the signature-operator orientation

\mathrm{sign}_{K}(M)=2^{-\lfloor n/2\rfloor}[D^{\operatorname{sign}}_{M}]\in{\operatorname{K}}^{\operatorname{an}}_{n}(M)[\mbox{$\frac{1}{2}$}].

Proof.

Let
{\operatorname{KO}}
denote the
8
-periodic ring spectrum representing real K-theory
and
{\operatorname{K}}
the
2
-periodic ring spectrum representing complex K-theory.
The homotopy ring of
{\operatorname{K}}
is
\pi_{*}({\operatorname{K}})=\mathbb{Z}[\beta^{\pm 1}],

where
\beta
is the complex Bott element in degree
2
, i.e.

\beta
is represented by the reduced canonical complex line bundle

H-1\in\widetilde{{\operatorname{K}}}^{0}(S^{2})
.
On
\pi_{4}
, the complexification
c:{\operatorname{KO}}\to{\operatorname{K}}
induces multiplication by
2
,

c_{*}=2:\pi_{4}({\operatorname{KO}})=\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{Z}=\pi_{4}({\operatorname{K}})
.
Thus there does not exist an element in
\pi_{4}({\operatorname{KO}})
that maps to

\beta^{2}
. But after inverting
2
, such an element exists.
Let
a\in\pi_{4}({\operatorname{KO}})[\mbox{$\frac{1}{2}$}]
be the unique element with

(7.5)

c_{*}(a)=\beta^{2}.

The localization
{\operatorname{KO}}[\mbox{$\frac{1}{2}$}]
is a
4
-periodic ring spectrum
with homotopy ring

\pi_{*}({\operatorname{KO}})[\mbox{$\frac{1}{2}$}]=\mathbb{Z}[\mbox{$\frac{1}{2}$}][a^{\pm 1}].

Its connective cover
{\operatorname{ko}}[\mbox{$\frac{1}{2}$}]
has homotopy ring

\pi_{*}({\operatorname{ko}})[\mbox{$\frac{1}{2}$}]=\mathbb{Z}[\mbox{$\frac{1}{2}$}][a].

Let
\mathbb{L}(R)
denote the (projective) symmetric algebraic L-theory
spectrum of a commutative unital ring
R
with involution, introduced first by Ranicki.
Let

\kappa:{\operatorname{KO}}[\mbox{$\frac{1}{2}$}]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathbb{L}(\real)[\mbox{$\frac{1}{2}$}]

be the equivalence of
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-ring spectra
constructed by the first named author in

[
Ban25
, Prop. 2.1]
. It induces the ring isomorphism

\mathbb{Z}[\mbox{$\frac{1}{2}$}][a^{\pm 1}]\longrightarrow\mathbb{Z}[\mbox{$\frac{1}{2}$}][x^{\pm 1}],~a\mapsto x,

on homotopy rings, where
x
denotes the signature
1
generator.
Let
\operatorname{MSPL}
be the Thom spectrum associated to the bordism theory of oriented PL manifolds.
In
[
Ran
, p. 385, Prop. 15.8]
, Ranicki constructed a morphism of ring spectra

\sigma^{*}:\operatorname{MSPL}\longrightarrow\mathbb{L}(\mathbb{Z})

such that the resulting
\mathbb{L}(\mathbb{Z})
-homology fundamental class

[M]_{\mathbb{L}}:=\sigma^{*}[\operatorname{id}_{M}]\in\mathbb{L}(\mathbb{Z})_{n}(M)

of an
n
-dimensional closed oriented PL manifold
M

hits the Mishchenko-Ranicki symmetric signature

\sigma^{*}(M)=A[M]_{\mathbb{L}}\in L^{n}(\mathbb{Z}[\pi_{1}M])

under the assembly map

A:\mathbb{L}(\mathbb{Z})_{n}(M)\to L^{n}(\mathbb{Z}[\pi_{1}M]).

(Ranicki extended
\sigma^{*}
to a morphism of ring spectra

\operatorname{MSTOP}\to\mathbb{L}(\mathbb{Z})
in
[
Ran79
, p. 290]
,
but we shall not require this extension for the purposes of the present paper.)
Technically, we will work with
\sigma^{*}
as constructed by
Laures and McClure in
[
LM14
]
using ad-theories.
Their incarnation of
\sigma^{*}
is an
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-ring map
(
[
LM14
, 1.4]
).
The localization morphism
\mathbb{L}(\mathbb{Z})\to\mathbb{L}(\mathbb{Z})[\mbox{$\frac{1}{2}$}]

is a morphism of ring spectra. Thus its composition with
\sigma^{*}

is a morphism of ring spectra

\operatorname{MSPL}\to\mathbb{L}(\mathbb{Z})[\mbox{$\frac{1}{2}$}]
, which we shall also denote by
\sigma^{*}
.
By
[
Ran80
, p. 243]
,
\sigma^{*}

induces on homotopy groups the map

\sigma^{*}_{\operatorname{pt}}:\Omega^{\operatorname{SPL}}_{4k}(\operatorname{pt})=\operatorname{MSPL}_{4k}(\operatorname{pt})\longrightarrow\mathbb{L}(\mathbb{Z})[\mbox{$\frac{1}{2}$}]_{4k}=\mathbb{Z}[\mbox{$\frac{1}{2}$}]\langle x^{k}\rangle

given by

(7.6)

\sigma^{*}_{\operatorname{pt}}[M^{4k}]=\sigma(M)\cdot x^{k},

where
\sigma(M)\in\mathbb{Z}
denotes the signature of
M
.
Let

\Delta^{\circ}_{\operatorname{SPL}}:\operatorname{MSPL}\longrightarrow{\operatorname{KO}}[\mbox{$\frac{1}{2}$}]

denote the composition

\operatorname{MSPL}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sigma^{*}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathbb{L}[\mbox{$\frac{1}{2}$}]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\kappa^{-1}}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\operatorname{KO}}[\mbox{$\frac{1}{2}$}].

The first named author showed
in
[
Ban25
, Prop. 3.3]
that
\Delta^{\circ}_{\operatorname{SPL}}

is homotopic to Sullivan’s orientation
\Delta_{\operatorname{SPL}}
.
As pointed out in
[
Ban25
, Cor. 3.4]
,
this implies in particular that the Sullivan orientation is homotopic
to a morphism of homotopy ring spectra.
Under the canonical morphism
\operatorname{MSO}\to\operatorname{MSPL}
,
which is a morphism of homotopy ring spectra,

\Delta^{\circ}_{\operatorname{SPL}}
restricts to a morphism of homotopy ring spectra

\Delta^{\circ}_{\operatorname{SO}}:\operatorname{MSO}\longrightarrow{\operatorname{KO}}[\mbox{$\frac{1}{2}$}]

that is homotopic to Sullivan’s
\Delta_{\operatorname{SO}}
.
As
\operatorname{MSO}
is connective,
\Delta^{\circ}_{\operatorname{SO}}
lifts, uniquely up to
homotopy, to the connective cover
{\operatorname{ko}}[\mbox{$\frac{1}{2}$}]
. The lift

\operatorname{MSO}\rightarrow{\operatorname{ko}}[\mbox{$\frac{1}{2}$}]

will again be denoted by
\Delta^{\circ}_{\operatorname{SO}}
and is
a morphism of homotopy ring spectra
(
[
Rud98
, p. 93, Thm. 4.28.(ii)]
).
Let

\mathcal{L}_{AS}:\operatorname{MSO}\longrightarrow{\operatorname{ko}}[\mbox{$\frac{1}{2}$}]

be the map of
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-rings constructed
by Land, Nikolaus and Schlichting in

[
LNS23
, Theorem 8.5]
.
Within maps of
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-rings,
\mathcal{L}_{AS}

is characterized uniquely by the homomorphism it induces
on homotopy rings, which is given by

[M^{4k}]\mapsto 2^{-2k}\sigma(M)a^{k}.

We claim that
\Psi^{2}\circ\mathcal{L}_{AS}
is homotopic to

\Delta^{\circ}_{\operatorname{SO}}
.
In order to see this, we verify first that both induce the same homomorphism
on homotopy groups:
It is well-known that the Sullivan orientation on homotopy
groups is given by the signature. More precisely,

\Delta_{{\operatorname{SO}}*}[M^{4k}]=\sigma(M)\cdot a^{k}.

The stable Adams operation acts on powers of
a
by

(7.7)

\Psi^{2}(a^{k})=4^{k}\cdot a^{k}.

Therefore,

\displaystyle(\Psi^{2}\mathcal{L}_{AS})_{*}[M^{4k}]

\displaystyle=\Psi^{2}_{*}(2^{-2k}\sigma(M)a^{k})=2^{-2k}\sigma(M)\Psi^{2}_{*}(a^{k})

\displaystyle=2^{-2k}\sigma(M)4^{k}a^{k}=\sigma(M)a^{k}=\Delta_{{\operatorname{SO}}*}[M^{4k}]=\Delta^{\circ}_{{\operatorname{SO}}*}[M^{4k}].

According to
[
LNS23
, Theorem 8.5]
,
the map

\pi_{0}\operatorname{Map}^{\operatorname{HoRing}}_{\operatorname{Sp}}(\operatorname{MSO},{\operatorname{ko}}[\mbox{$\frac{1}{2}$}])\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi_{*}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{Ring}}(\operatorname{MSO}_{*},{\operatorname{ko}}[\mbox{$\frac{1}{2}$}]_{*}),

where
\pi_{0}\operatorname{Map}^{\operatorname{HoRing}}_{\operatorname{Sp}}

denotes the connected components of the space of maps of spectra that
are homotopy ring maps,
is injective. Both
\Psi^{2}\circ\mathcal{L}_{AS}
and

\Delta^{\circ}_{\operatorname{SO}}
are homotopy ring maps and we have seen that

\pi_{*}(\Psi^{2}\circ\mathcal{L}_{AS})=\pi_{*}(\Delta^{\circ}_{\operatorname{SO}})
.
Thus by injectivity of
\pi_{*}
,

\Psi^{2}\circ\mathcal{L}_{AS}
is homotopic to

\Delta^{\circ}_{\operatorname{SO}}
, establishing the claim.

Thm. 8.5 of
loc cit
also states that the composition

\operatorname{MSO}_{*}(X)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{L}_{AS}}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\operatorname{ko}}[\mbox{$\frac{1}{2}$}]_{*}(X)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle c}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\operatorname{k}}[\mbox{$\frac{1}{2}$}]_{*}(X)

sends
[f:M^{n}\to X]
to

2^{-\lfloor n/2\rfloor}f_{*}[D^{\operatorname{sign}}_{M}]
. Since

\mathcal{L}_{AS}\simeq(\Psi^{2})^{-1}\circ\Delta^{\circ}_{\operatorname{SO}}\simeq(\Psi^{2})^{-1}\circ\Delta_{\operatorname{SO}},

and thus

c\circ\mathcal{L}_{AS}\simeq c\circ(\Psi^{2})^{-1}\circ\Delta_{\operatorname{SO}},

it follows that

2^{-\lfloor n/2\rfloor}[D^{\operatorname{sign}}_{M}]=(c\circ\mathcal{L}_{AS})[\operatorname{id}_{M}]=(c\circ(\Psi^{2})^{-1}\circ\Delta_{\operatorname{SO}})[\operatorname{id}_{M}]=(c\circ(\Psi^{2})^{-1})(\Delta_{\operatorname{SO}}(M)).

∎

7.3.
The Case of Singular Witt Spaces.

Our goal now is to extend the above arguments to the case of Witt pseudomanifolds,
which includes all pure-dimensional complex algebraic varieties.

Let
Z=\mathbb{Z}[\mbox{$\frac{1}{2}$}]
denote the ring of integers localized
at odd primes.
The Laurent polynomial ring
R:=Z[t,t^{-1}]

is a
\mathbb{Z}
-graded ring with
\deg(t)=4
.
There is a canonical subring inclusion

Z[t]\subset R,~t\mapsto t.

Let
\mathbb{C}P^{2}
be complex projective space of complex dimension
2
.
Denote Witt bordism away from
2
by

W_{*}(X):=\Omega^{\operatorname{Witt}}_{*}(X)\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}Z.

(Recall that by Proposition
4.5
,
we may assume that representatives
of
\Omega^{\operatorname{Witt}}_{*}(X)
are smoothly stratified and thus possess a well-defined
signature operator.)
This is a
\mathbb{Z}
-graded homology theory with coefficients

W_{*}(\operatorname{pt})=Z[c],\quad c:=[\mathbb{C}P^{2}\to\operatorname{pt}]\otimes 1\in W_{4}(\operatorname{pt}).

(After inverting
2
, only the signature survives as an
invariant; the
2
- and
4
-torsion is removed.)
The
\mathbb{Z}
-graded abelian group
W_{*}(X)
is a right module over
the ring
W_{*}(\operatorname{pt})
as usual. The ring isomorphism

Z[t]\to W_{*}(\operatorname{pt})
induced by

t\mapsto c\otimes 1\in W_{4}(\operatorname{pt})

makes
W_{*}(X)
into a right
Z[t]
-module. Thus
t
acts on

[f:V\to X]\otimes r\in W_{n}(X)
by

([f]\otimes r)\cdot t=[V\times\mathbb{C}P^{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\operatorname{pr}_{1}}}{{\longrightarrow}}V\stackrel{{\scriptstyle f}}{{\longrightarrow}}X]\otimes r.

We may then form the tensor product

\overline{W}_{*}(X):=W_{*}(X)\otimes_{Z[t]}R,

which is
\mathbb{Z}
-graded by

\deg(x\otimes_{Z[t]}rt^{k})=n+4k,

x\in W_{n}(X),

r\in Z.

It is shown in
[
Ban25
]
that
\overline{W}_{*}(-)
is a
homology theory.
It is naturally a right
R
-module and right multiplication
with
t
is an isomorphism with inverse given by right multiplication
with
t^{-1}
. This shows that
\overline{W}_{*}(-)
is
4
-periodic
and we refer to it as
periodic Witt-bordism at odd primes
.
The inclusion
Z[t]\subset R
induces a natural map

i_{*}:W_{*}(X)=W_{*}(X)\otimes_{Z[t]}Z[t]\longrightarrow\overline{W}_{*}(X).

As noted above, the homotopy ring of the
ring spectrum
{\operatorname{K}}
is
\pi_{*}({\operatorname{K}})=\mathbb{Z}[\beta^{\pm 1}],

where
\beta
is the complex Bott element in degree
2
.
Away from
2
,
\pi_{*}({\operatorname{K}}[\mbox{$\frac{1}{2}$}])=Z[\beta^{\pm 1}]
.
As
{\operatorname{K}}[\mbox{$\frac{1}{2}$}]
is a ring spectrum, the groups

({\operatorname{K}}[\mbox{$\frac{1}{2}$}])_{*}(X)={\operatorname{K}}^{\operatorname{top}}_{*}(X)[\mbox{$\frac{1}{2}$}]

come with a canonical right
(K[\mbox{$\frac{1}{2}$}])_{*}(\operatorname{pt})
-module structure.
Via the ring homomorphism

R=Z[t,t^{-1}]\longrightarrow Z[\beta,\beta^{-1}],~t\mapsto\mbox{$\frac{1}{4}$}\beta^{2},

we make
({\operatorname{K}}[\mbox{$\frac{1}{2}$}])_{*}(X)
into a right
R
-module

(({\operatorname{K}}[\mbox{$\frac{1}{2}$}])_{*}(X),+,\bullet)
.
Thus
t\in R
acts on
y\in({\operatorname{K}}[\mbox{$\frac{1}{2}$}])_{*}(X)
by

y\bullet t:=y\cdot\mbox{$\frac{1}{4}$}\beta^{2}.

Let
\operatorname{MWITT}
denote the (Quinn-type) ring spectrum associated to the
multiplicative ad-theory of Witt spaces, representing Witt bordism
(see Banagl-Laures-McClure
[
BLM19
]
),
and let

\Delta:\operatorname{MWITT}\longrightarrow{\operatorname{KO}}[\mbox{$\frac{1}{2}$}]

denote the second author’s ring-spectrum level Siegel-Sullivan orientation,

[
Ban25
]
. It restricts to the Sullivan
orientation
\Delta:\operatorname{MSPL}\to{\operatorname{KO}}[\mbox{$\frac{1}{2}$}]
under the canonical
map
\operatorname{MSPL}\to\operatorname{MWITT}
.
By
[
Ban25
, Prop. 5.7]
, the induced natural transformation

\Delta_{*}:\Omega^{\operatorname{Witt}}_{*}(-)\to{\operatorname{KO}}_{*}(-)[\mbox{$\frac{1}{2}$}]

of homology theories agrees with Siegel’s transformation
\mu^{\operatorname{Witt}}

as described in
[
Sie83
]
.
On homotopy groups,
\Delta
induces the homomorphism

\Delta_{*}:\Omega^{\operatorname{Witt}}_{4k}=\operatorname{MWITT}_{4k}\longrightarrow{\operatorname{KO}}[\mbox{$\frac{1}{2}$}]_{4k}=Z\langle a^{k}\rangle

given by

(7.8)

\Delta_{*}[V^{4k}\to\operatorname{pt}]=\sigma(V)\cdot a^{k},

where
\sigma(V)\in\mathbb{Z}
is the signature of the intersection form
on the intersection homology groups
IH_{2k}(V;\mathbb{Q})
of
V
.
The Siegel-Sullivan orientation class of a compact
n
-dimensional Witt space

(V,\partial V)
is given by the image

\Delta(V):=\Delta_{*}[\operatorname{id}_{V}]\in{\operatorname{KO}}_{n}(V,\partial V)[\mbox{$\frac{1}{2}$}]

of the Witt bordism class of the identity on
V
.
Let

\gamma:W_{*}(X)\longrightarrow({\operatorname{K}}[\mbox{$\frac{1}{2}$}])_{*}(X)={\operatorname{K}}^{\operatorname{top}}_{*}(X)[\mbox{$\frac{1}{2}$}]

be the natural transformation of homology theories given by

\gamma([f:V\to X]\otimes r):=r\cdot c(\Psi^{2})^{-1}f_{*}\Delta(V)=r\cdot c(\Psi^{2})^{-1}\Delta_{*}[f],~r\in Z.

Lemma 7.3
.

The homomorphism
\gamma:W_{*}(X)\longrightarrow{\operatorname{K}}^{\operatorname{top}}_{*}(X)[\mbox{$\frac{1}{2}$}]

is
Z[t]
-linear.

Proof.

Let
[f:V^{n}\to X]
be any element of
W_{n}(X)
.
As
\Delta:\operatorname{MWITT}\to{\operatorname{KO}}[\mbox{$\frac{1}{2}$}]
is a morphism of ring spectra,
the diagram

\textstyle{\Omega^{\operatorname{Witt}}_{*}(X)\otimes\Omega^{\operatorname{Witt}}_{*}(\operatorname{pt})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Delta_{*}\otimes\Delta_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{mult}}
\textstyle{\Omega^{\operatorname{Witt}}_{*}(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Delta_{*}}
\textstyle{({\operatorname{KO}}[\mbox{$\frac{1}{2}$}])_{*}(X)\otimes({\operatorname{KO}}[\mbox{$\frac{1}{2}$}])_{*}(\operatorname{pt})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{mult}}
\textstyle{({\operatorname{KO}}[\mbox{$\frac{1}{2}$}])_{*}(X)}

commutes and hence

\displaystyle\Delta_{*}([f]\cdot t)

\displaystyle=\Delta_{*}([f]\cdot[\mathbb{C}P^{2}\to\operatorname{pt}])=\Delta_{*}[f]\cdot\Delta_{*}[\mathbb{C}P^{2}\to\operatorname{pt}]

\displaystyle=\Delta_{*}[f]\cdot\sigma(\mathbb{C}P^{2})a=\Delta_{*}[f]\cdot a.

In view of (
7.5
) and (
7.7
),
we find that

c(\Psi^{2})^{-1}(a)=c(\mbox{$\frac{1}{4}$}a)=\mbox{$\frac{1}{4}$}\beta^{2}.

Since
(\Psi^{2})^{-1}
and
c
are ring maps as well, we conclude

\displaystyle\gamma([f]\cdot t)

\displaystyle=c(\Psi^{2})^{-1}\Delta_{*}([f]\cdot t)=c(\Psi^{2})^{-1}((\Delta_{*}[f])\cdot a)

\displaystyle=(c(\Psi^{2})^{-1}\Delta_{*}[f])\cdot c(\Psi^{2})^{-1}(a)=\gamma[f]\cdot\mbox{$\frac{1}{4}$}\beta^{2}=\gamma[f]\bullet t.

∎

Recall that in (
4.3
)
of Section
4.2
, we had defined a natural transformation

\theta:W_{n}(X)=\Omega^{\operatorname{Witt}}_{n}(X)[\mbox{$\frac{1}{2}$}]\cong\Omega^{{\operatorname{Witt}},\infty}_{n}(X)[\mbox{$\frac{1}{2}$}]\to{\operatorname{K}}^{\operatorname{an}}_{n}(X)[\mbox{$\frac{1}{2}$}]

of homology theories given by

\theta([f:W^{n}\to X]\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}r):=r2^{-\lfloor n/2\rfloor}f_{*}[D^{\operatorname{sign}}_{W}]=rf_{*}(\mathrm{sign}_{K}(W)).

Lemma 7.4
.

The homomorphism
\theta:W_{*}(X)\longrightarrow{\operatorname{K}}^{\operatorname{an}}_{*}(X)[\mbox{$\frac{1}{2}$}]

is
Z[t]
-linear.

Proof.

Let
\mathbb{P}^{2}=\mathbb{C}P^{2}
denote complex projective space
and let
\operatorname{const}:\mathbb{P}^{2}\to\operatorname{pt}
be the constant map.
The image of the signature operator class

[D^{\operatorname{sign}}_{\mathbb{P}^{2}}]\in{\operatorname{K}}_{4}(\mathbb{P}^{2})[\mbox{$\frac{1}{2}$}]
under

\operatorname{const}_{*}:{\operatorname{K}}_{4}(\mathbb{P}^{2})[\mbox{$\frac{1}{2}$}]\to{\operatorname{K}}_{4}(\operatorname{pt})[\mbox{$\frac{1}{2}$}]

is the index (multiplied by the corresponding Bott generator)

\operatorname{const}_{*}[D^{\operatorname{sign}}_{\mathbb{P}^{2}}]=\sigma(\mathbb{P}^{2})\cdot\beta^{2}=\beta^{2}\in{\operatorname{K}}_{4}(\operatorname{pt})[\mbox{$\frac{1}{2}$}].

Let
[f:V^{n}\to X]
be any element of
W_{n}(X)
.
By Corollary
5.18
,

[D^{\operatorname{sign}}_{V\times\mathbb{P}^{2}}]=[D^{\operatorname{sign}}_{V}]\boxtimes[D^{\operatorname{sign}}_{\mathbb{P}^{2}}].

(There is no factor of
2
here, since the dimension of
\mathbb{P}^{2}
is even.)
Consequently,

\displaystyle\theta([f]\cdot t)

\displaystyle=\theta([f\times\operatorname{const}:V\times\mathbb{P}^{2}\to X\times\operatorname{pt}=X])

\displaystyle=2^{-\lfloor\frac{n+4}{2}\rfloor}(f\times\operatorname{const})_{*}[D^{\operatorname{sign}}_{V\times\mathbb{P}^{2}}]=2^{-\lfloor\frac{n}{2}\rfloor}\cdot\mbox{$\frac{1}{4}$}(f\times\operatorname{const})_{*}([D^{\operatorname{sign}}_{V}]\boxtimes[D^{\operatorname{sign}}_{\mathbb{P}^{2}}])

\displaystyle=2^{-\lfloor\frac{n}{2}\rfloor}f_{*}[D^{\operatorname{sign}}_{V}]\cdot\mbox{$\frac{1}{4}$}\operatorname{const}_{*}[D^{\operatorname{sign}}_{\mathbb{P}^{2}}]=2^{-\lfloor\frac{n}{2}\rfloor}f_{*}[D^{\operatorname{sign}}_{V}]\cdot\mbox{$\frac{1}{4}$}\beta^{2}

\displaystyle=(\theta[f])\bullet t.

∎

The following Proposition generalizes

[
Ban25
, Prop. 5.6]
from maps into

({\operatorname{KO}}\mbox{$\frac{1}{2}$})_{*}(X)
to maps into any
Z[t,t^{-1}]
-module
M
;
see also
[
BCS03
, Prop. 2, p. 597]

of Cappell, Shaneson and the second named author.
The proof follows
[
Ban25
]
, but we provide details
for the reader’s convenience.

Proposition 7.5
.

Let
X
be a compact PL space and
M
a right
Z[t,t^{-1}]
-module.

1. Given a
Z[t]
-linear map

\alpha:W_{*}(X)\to M

there exists a unique extension of
\alpha
to a homomorphism

\overline{\alpha}:\overline{W}_{*}(X)\longrightarrow M

of
Z[t,t^{-1}]
-modules.

2. Let
\alpha,\beta:W_{*}(X)\to M

be
Z[t]
-linear maps. If

\alpha([g:N\to X]\otimes 1)=\beta([g]\otimes 1)
for
every
g
on smooth manifolds
N
, then

\alpha=\beta
on
W_{*}(X)
, and

\overline{\alpha}_{*}=\overline{\beta}_{*}

on
\overline{W}_{*}(X)
.

Proof.

We prove statement 1:
We denote the scalar multiplication in
M
by
\bullet
.
Let

A:W_{*}(X)\times Z[t,t^{-1}]\longrightarrow M

be the map given by

A(w,p):=\alpha(w)\bullet p.

We regard
Z[t,t^{-1}]
as a left module over
Z[t]
.
For
q\in Z[t],
the
Z[t]
-linearity of
\alpha
implies that

\displaystyle A(wq,p)

\displaystyle=\alpha(wq)\bullet p=(\alpha(w)\bullet q)\bullet p

\displaystyle=\alpha(w)\bullet(qp)=A(w,qp).

Therefore,
A
is
Z[t]
-bilinear and hence, by the universal
property of the tensor product
\otimes_{Z[t]}
,
induces a well-defined homomorphism

\overline{\alpha}:W_{*}(X)\otimes_{Z[t]}Z[t,t^{-1}]\longrightarrow M

of abelian groups such that

\overline{\alpha}(w\otimes_{Z[t]}p)=A(w,p)=\alpha(w)\bullet p
.
Then
\overline{\alpha}_{*}
is
Z[t,t^{-1}]
-linear, as for

p,p^{\prime}\in Z[t,t^{-1}],

\displaystyle\overline{\alpha}((w\otimes_{Z[t]}p)\cdot p^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\overline{\alpha}((w\otimes_{Z[t]}(pp^{\prime}))=\alpha(w)\bullet(pp^{\prime})

\displaystyle=(\alpha(w)\bullet p)\bullet p^{\prime}=\overline{\alpha}(w\otimes_{Z[t]}p)\bullet p^{\prime},

and the diagram

(7.9)

\textstyle{W_{*}(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha}
\scriptstyle{i_{*}}
\textstyle{\overline{W}_{*}(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\overline{\alpha}}
\textstyle{M}

commutes.

We turn to the proof of uniqueness.
Suppose that
\alpha^{\prime}:\overline{W}_{*}(X)\to M

is any
Z[t,t^{-1}]
-linear extension of
\alpha
,
i.e.
\alpha^{\prime}\circ i_{*}=\alpha
. Then

\displaystyle\alpha^{\prime}(w\otimes_{Z[t]}p)

\displaystyle=\alpha^{\prime}(w\otimes_{Z[t]}(1\cdot p))=\alpha^{\prime}((w\otimes_{Z[t]}1)\cdot p)

\displaystyle=\alpha^{\prime}(w\otimes_{Z[t]}1)\bullet p=(\alpha^{\prime}i_{*}(w))\bullet p

\displaystyle=\alpha(w)\bullet p=\overline{\alpha}(w\otimes_{Z[t]}p).

Hence
\overline{\alpha}
is unique.

We prove statement 2:
Since
\alpha
and
\beta
are
Z[t]
-linear, they induce
uniquely
Z[t,t^{-1}]
-linear transformations

\overline{\alpha},\overline{\beta}:(W_{*}(X)\otimes_{Z[t]}Z[t,t^{-1}])_{j}\longrightarrow M,~j\in\mathbb{Z},

as explained in statement 1.
For an integer
j
, let
\bar{j}
denote its residue class in
\mathbb{Z}/_{4}
.
On the groups

C_{\bar{j}}(X):=\bigoplus_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\Omega^{\operatorname{SO}}_{j+4k}(X)[\tfrac{1}{2}]
,
define an equivalence relation by

[P^{j+4k}\times N^{4i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\operatorname{proj}}}{{\longrightarrow}}P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle f}}{{\longrightarrow}}X]\sim\sigma(N)\cdot[P^{j+4k}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle f}}{{\longrightarrow}}X].

(See also
[
KL05
, p. 193]
.)
Let
Q_{\bar{j}}(X,Y):=C_{\bar{j}}(X,Y)/\sim
denote the corresponding
quotient. One can show that
Q_{*}(-)

is a (
\mathbb{Z}/_{4}
-graded) homology theory on compact PL pairs, see

[
Ban25
]
.
For any
j\in\mathbb{Z}
, a well-defined map

\omega:Q_{\bar{j}}(X)\otimes Z\longrightarrow(W_{*}(X)\otimes_{Z[t]}Z[t,t^{-1}])_{j}

is given by setting

\omega([g:N^{j-4k}\to X]\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}r)=[g]\otimes_{Z[t]}rt^{k}\in W_{j-4k}(X)\otimes_{Z[t]}Z\langle t^{k}\rangle,~k\in\mathbb{Z},~r\in Z,

where one views the closed oriented smooth manifold
N

as a Witt space via its canonical PL structure.
This map is an isomorphism on compact PL spaces
X

as was shown in
[
Ban25
]
.
Therefore, Witt bordism classes are representable by smooth manifolds
away from
2
.

Given an element

[f:V^{j-4k}\to X]\otimes_{Z[t]}rt^{k}\in(W_{*}(X)\otimes_{Z[t]}Z[t,t^{-1}])_{j}=\overline{W}_{j}(X)

k\in\mathbb{Z},

r\in Z
,
there exists a (unique) element
q\in Q_{\bar{j}}(X)\otimes Z

with
\omega(q)=[f]\otimes_{Z[t]}rt^{k}
,
as
\omega
is an isomorphism.
Such an element is represented in the quotient
Q_{\bar{j}}(X)\otimes Z

by an element of the form

q=\sum_{i=1}^{m}[g_{i}:M_{i}^{j-4k_{i}}\to X]\otimes r_{i},~[g_{i}]\in\Omega^{\operatorname{SO}}_{j-4k_{i}}(X),~r_{i}\in Z,~k_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}.

By the definition of
\omega,

\omega([g_{i}]\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}r_{i})=[g_{i}]\otimes_{Z[t]}r_{i}t^{k_{i}},

so that

[f]\otimes_{Z[t]}rt^{k}=\sum_{i=1}^{m}[g_{i}]\otimes_{Z[t]}r_{i}t^{k_{i}}

and consequently,

\displaystyle\overline{\alpha}_{*}([f]\otimes_{Z[t]}rt^{k})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\overline{\alpha}_{*}([g_{i}]\otimes_{Z[t]}r_{i}t^{k_{i}})=\sum_{i=1}^{m}(\alpha_{*}[g_{i}])\cdot r_{i}a^{k_{i}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{m}(\beta_{*}[g_{i}])\cdot r_{i}a^{k_{i}}=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\overline{\beta}_{*}([g_{i}]\otimes_{Z[t]}r_{i}t^{k_{i}})=\overline{\beta}_{*}([f]\otimes_{Z[t]}rt^{k}).

This proves that the periodic versions agree on
\overline{W}_{*}(X)
,

\overline{\alpha}_{*}=\overline{\beta}_{*}
.
Using the commutativity of (
7.9
)
we deduce

\alpha_{*}=\overline{\alpha}_{*}\circ i_{*}=\overline{\beta}_{*}\circ i_{*}=\beta_{*}.

∎

Theorem 7.6
.

Let
X
be a closed Witt space and

\Delta(X)\in{\operatorname{KO}}^{\operatorname{top}}_{n}(X)[\mbox{$\frac{1}{2}$}]

its Siegel-Sullivan orientation,
[
Sie83
]
,
[
Ban25
]
.
Under the identification (
7.4
), the element

c(\Psi^{2})^{-1}\Delta(X)\in{\operatorname{K}}^{\operatorname{top}}_{n}(X)[\mbox{$\frac{1}{2}$}]

of an
n
-dimensional closed smoothly stratified Witt space
X

corresponds to the signature-operator orientation

\mathrm{sign}_{K}(X)=2^{-\lfloor n/2\rfloor}[D^{\operatorname{sign}}_{X}]\in{\operatorname{K}}^{\operatorname{an}}_{n}(X)[\mbox{$\frac{1}{2}$}].

Proof.

The identification (
7.4
)
is
Z[t]
-linear.
Thus, under this identification, we have homomorphisms

\gamma,\theta:W_{*}(X)\longrightarrow K^{\operatorname{top}}_{*}(X)[\mbox{$\frac{1}{2}$}],

which are both
Z[t]
-linear by Lemmas

7.3
and
7.4
.
The two homomorphisms agree on smooth manifolds
according to Theorem
7.2
.
Proposition
7.5
then implies
that
\gamma=\theta
on
W_{*}(X)

(where we have used the standard identification of analytic and topological K-homology, (
7.4
)).
∎
